Spit (a creature action, not a spell): Parryable, E1, 350 / projectile / 600, 3% plus Stung, a projectile at the Echelon 1 tier. Mix-up: it spits at a player standing still to forage beside it. Counter: parry it, or forage from the other side.
Drops: blister gel (pickling agent, Sharp), seed-fat (Fat, edible when rendered), no crystals.
Teaches: Foraging, which is written for this plant; Range for popping a cluster from distance.
Counterplay: CM: one cheap E1 at the far end. SM: a Slash wave from range. VB, VF: a thrown weapon or rock pops them; a Fist who reads the spacing walks the gaps untouched. Contact ignores Guard, so never Guard through a cluster.
Sap Ooze (Awakening to Apprentice band). A translucent amber slime the size of a dog that leaves a glossy trail and splits when struck hard. Overgrown states, cavern floors and Bleed Zones, drawn to leyline sap, in groups of 2 to 4; cold dissolves it, so frozen states have none. Slow and relentless, it follows its own trails, and halves merge when they touch.
Engulf: Grab, E1, 560 / 150 / 700 whiff, 4% then 3 contact ticks while held, capsule 3. Mix-up: a half you ignored Engulfs you from behind. Counter: hit it in startup; never let halves merge behind you.
Lash: Parryable, E1, 330 / 100 / 450, 4%, whip 5. Mix-up: it lashes from inside its Trail, so a Tempo step-in puts you on acid. Counter: parry and step back, or burn the Trail first (fire ignites sap).
Trail: contact, E1, persists 10s, 3% per 0.5s standing in it, 1 wide. Mix-up: laid across the only dry path. Counter: dash or Step i-frames cross it, or wait it out.
Split: any hit of 8% or more spawns two smaller, faster halves. Mix-up: a mage's E2 makes two enemies out of one. Counter: E1 hits do not split it; many small hits beat one big one.
Drops: sap gel (pickling agent, Sweet), amber bead (common glyph material), Mana Crystals at 10% for 1.
Teaches: Efficiency, since cheap spells win; Footwork, dodging through the Lash (5.19.4).
Counterplay: CM: E1 chip from range, never E2; kite on dry ground. SM: Slash waves (E1) do not split it. VB: Strikes, not Techniques (Techniques are E2). VF: Strikes and throws; a half thrown into deep water dissolves.
Lantern Nettle (Apprentice band). A walking vine-knot carrying a glowing seed-lantern that looks like a lit campfire from a distance. Bleed Zones at night and overgrown states: 11.5's deceptive flora with legs, drawing the hungry. It stands still until approached within 10, then lashes; below a third of its mass it flees toward the Bleed Zone's heart.
Vine Lash: Parryable, E1, 340 / 100 / 480, 5% plus Stung, whip 7. Mix-up: it lashes from 7, beyond its apparent reach, at a player walking to the "campfire". Counter: parry; a parried Nettle drops its lantern for 2s and shows what it is.
Root Snare: Grab, E1, 550 / 150 / 700 whiff, 3% and Root, circle 2 at your feet. Mix-up: Snare, then Burst while you are rooted. Counter: Dash off the circle; rooted, you can still Block the Burst, taking a Reel and a little chip but no Guard Break, since the Nettle has no second heavy hit.
Lantern Burst: Unparryable, E2, 700 / 150 / 900, 10% plus Stung, radius 5. Mix-up: used as cover as it turns to flee. Counter: walk out of 5; the recovery is a free hit on a fleeing plant.
Drops: nettle acid (pickling agent, Bitter; the overworld's learnable poison source, 18.14), lantern seed (a glowing cosmetic hearth fuel), Mana Crystals at 20% for 1.
Teaches: Foraging and Palate (the seed is Bitter beneath); the Footwork catalyst "dodge three Unparryable attacks in one fight" fits a pair.
Counterplay: CM: range. SM: Slash waves at 7 outrange the Lash. VB: Guard does nothing against contact; parry the Lash, Step the Snare, Technique in Burst recovery. VF: the Snare disables Step, so read the circle; a Strike breaks the Snare's startup.
14.6 Spellcasters (LOCKED, v3.3)
Driftwalker (Apprentice to Adept band by variant). A human exile in weather-grey layers with a faction mark burned off the sleeve; the aura flickers because they stopped training Suppression when they left. Everywhere the Drift goes: camps of 2 to 4 in ruin and grassland states, singletons in Bleed Zones. Each faction (11.11) produces its own exiles, which sets the element and spell list. It fights like a player of its band: zones with E1, commits E2 at mid range, Barriers when pressed, cancels (paying the feint tax), Contests when it is winning the lock point and releases when it is not, and flees toward camp at low mana. Each variant teaches 2 to 3 spell ids; a camp covers 5 to 8.
Example kit (a fire exile, Apprentice): e1_firebolt, e2_fireball, e0_barrier, e0_cinder and e0_gust with their 4.13 data, plus Knife: Parryable, E1, 300 / 80 / 450, 3%, thrust 3.
Firebolt. Mix-up: it cancels a Firebolt to bait a parry whiff, paying 50%, and can afford it twice. Counter: a cancelled cast still shows recovery; counter-hit it.
Fireball. Mix-up: aimed at your feet, so the Unparryable splash lands even when the projectile is dodged. Counter: intercept it with an E1, or clash it with an E2 for the Last Word, far from you (4.8).
Barrier. Mix-up: raised as you commit a Breaker Technique, so it drains 1.5x but you stand in recovery. Counter: alternate Grabs (which ignore Barrier) with Breakers.
Knife. Mix-up: out of mana, it draws a knife. Counter: an E1 Strike with nothing behind it; a Hollow Driftwalker is the Exhaustion lesson shown on someone else.
Drops: travel rations (Plain band, safe, Hearty), a cheap Tome (3%, one of its taught spells), glyph material of its element, Mana Crystals at 70% for 1 to 3 (its own assets).
Teaches: every spell on its list by Suffering (Echelon 2 only at or above your Power Score, 5.20); Durability on Contest wins; the Cast Speed catalyst "punish a parry against a stronger foe"; Reading the Blow for Blades (its Knife).
Counterplay: CM: a mirror match, and the game's PvP tutor. SM: close through the volley; it backs up toward its camp. VB: Spellcut E1s on the way in, Breaker into Barrier, Grab a turtle. VF: the Iron Palm catalyst "close from maximum range to a caster through a volley without being hit" is written against a Driftwalker camp.
Glyph Heron (Apprentice band). A wading bird twice a person's height with faint sigils inscribed in its feathers; it stands in shallows and casts with its beak. Flooded and ocean states, river mouths and rain fronts, in flocks of 2 to 3 that keep their distance. A kiting caster: Tidebolts from range, a Torrent at mid range, and Lift when you close.
Tidebolt (e1_tidebolt, 4.13). Mix-up: three Herons stagger bolts, so clashing one leaves two coming. Counter: Block the volley (E1 never Reels or chips), or close.
Torrent (e2_torrent, 4.13). Mix-up: fired along the shallows you wade through slowly, and its 45-degree-per-second sweep covers a slow wade. Counter: a lateral dash or a clash; Spellcut works on Breakers.
Wing Buffet: Unparryable, E1, 400 / 120 / 600, 5% plus knockback, arc 180 degrees, 4. Mix-up: it knocks you back into the Torrent's shallows. Counter: Dash through it, or Block (E1: no Reel, no chip).
Lift: mobility (flight), 500ms; Grounding applies (4.10). Mix-up: it lifts over water you cannot follow and resumes bolts. Counter: any E2 or Technique grounds it; a Fist Leaps and Grabs it.
Drops: Heron meat (Hearty; Cold if a water front crossed), sigil feather (Glyph of Expansion base), Tome fragment (water family, rare), Mana Crystals at 30% for 1.
Teaches: water Echelons 1 and 2 by Suffering (with the Power Score rule for 2); Range on a flier; Grounding practice before guards use nets (12.3).
Counterplay: CM: clash the Torrent with an E2; Tidebolts are chip. SM: Wind Slash cannot ground it (E1); use an Ice Lance or a thrown blade Technique. VB: Spellcut the Torrent once you know a Technique (4.12), at heavy durability, so the fight is a durability budget. VF: the hardest overworld kiter; Leap-Grab is the answer and the lesson.
The Bound Archivist (Master band). A desiccated mage fused into a stone reading chair, ringed by orbiting pages; the chair is a construct, the mage is not. Pressure Peaks rated Echelon 2 or 3 (11.3), the deepest room of Risen Ruins, and Falling Star craters on a rare roll; one per shard at a time. A stationary Calamity caster: it opens with Echelon 2 to test you and holds Echelon 3 for when you commit. It cannot move; the chair turns. The room is the fight: cover, pillars and a Pressure field. It teaches one Calamity at a time, rotating with the Drift state (e3_sunfall this block, e3_cairnfall the next).
Page Shards (e1_shard, e1_firebolt or e1_arc by block). Mix-up: shards orbit before they fire, so the launch direction is not the orbit direction. Counter: Block; they are chip meant to drain mana before the Index.
Chapter (e2_torrent or e2_rimewall). Mix-up: a wall rises between you as the Index begins, hiding the projectile until late. Counter: walls break under E2, or stand where the wall is not.
Index (e3_sunfall, e3_shardstorm, e3_cairnfall or e3_unmaking by block, with the spell's data and world-scale telegraph). Mix-up: cast while you are mid-Contest with a Chapter. Counter: break off (Dash) and read the sky tell; parrying the projectile costs mana (4.6), dodging the area is free. Surviving it grants full Insight.
Turn: 600 / - / 400, the chair rotates to face you. Mix-up: none; it is the weakness. Counter: cross behind it for 400ms of its back; it cannot Index while turning.
Drops: Tome fragments of the block's Calamity family (a fragment source, 5.20), a sealed grain jar nearby (18.7), rare glyph materials, Mana Crystals 3 to 6. Threat is high for everyone below Master, so its XP is real once.
Teaches: a Calamity by Suffering; Pressure Tolerance; the Cast Speed and Durability catalysts at the stronger-foe bar; the Horizon Cut Art's test target is the Index. Vowed characters are checked against the Body threshold for the room's rating (11.3): at or above it they stand unpressured, below it they take 50%.
Counterplay: CM: a Contest and range duel at Master band; clash Chapters, dodge Index areas, and parry Index projectiles only when the mana is there. SM: approach under pillars, cut the Shards, close during Turn. VB: Spellcut E1 on the way in and E2 once you know a Technique; Horizon Cut splits the Index. VF: a Fist at the room's Body threshold walks in unpressured, so this is the one Master fight a Fist enters more easily than a mage; Step the Index area, one Step per Index.
Coverage rule (LOCKED, v3.3). 5.20's PvE source rule is enforced by data, not memory:
Every spell-table row (4.13) carries a required field pve_sources: [mob, NPC or Tome id]. A build with an empty field fails validation (the spirit of 4.11 Law 6: a spell with no source does not ship).
The Driftwalker roster is generated from the table: for each element at Echelons 1 and 2, variants whose teaches lists cover that element's spells, at most 3 per mob. A new spell generates a new variant, with no hand work. On the current table: fire (e1_firebolt, e2_fireball, e0_cinder), ice (e1_shard, e2_icelance, e0_frostmote), lightning (e1_arc), wind (e1_windslash, e0_gust), earth (e1_stonegrip), water (e2_torrent, e1_tidebolt), and any variant may carry e0_barrier and e0_manasense. Cantrips also have Anchor trainers (5.20), so they are covered twice.
Creatures cover the rest with existing ids; where none exists they cast a creature spell (4.13), never a creature-only spell. Creature actions such as the Writ, Spit and Boulder Toss are not spells and teach nothing.
Calamity sources: the Cairn Walker and the Anvilback (e3_cairnfall), the Unburied and the Likeness (e3_unmaking), the Gloamwing (e3_shardstorm), and the Bound Archivist and the Marshal (rotating). Distinct means distinct casters, so two casters of one spell both count, and three are always reachable on any shard.
Guards cast table spells, so they are Suffering sources at the guard rate (12.1).
14.7 Pressure Peak Beasts (LOCKED, v3.3)
Anvilback (Master band; an Echelon 2 Pressure Peak). A six-legged beast the size of a hall whose back is a leyline knot grown into an anvil of black stone; mana weather forms over it. One per mountain region, fixed in the bones (11.3); its Pressure varies with the Drift state. Indifferent until struck, then a siege; it never leaves the Peak. Its back is a platform: climbing it (vowed) or landing on it (mage) is the only way to the knot, where it takes real damage. Absorb on legs and flanks, none on the knot; earth element.
Leg Stamp: Unparryable, E2, 800 / 150 / 1100, 15%, radius 5 per leg. Mix-up: two legs can Stamp 400ms apart. Counter: each decal shows which leg; stand between legs, not under one.
Tail Sweep: Breaker, E2, 700 / 300 / 1000, 16%, arc 270 degrees, 12, 2 high. Mix-up: low and wide, it clips a climber on a leg. Counter: Leap or hop over it; never Guard it.
Shrug: Unparryable, E1, 500 / 200 / 800, 6% plus knock-off for anyone on its back, the back. Mix-up: it Shrugs the moment a climber reaches the knot. Counter: the shoulders roll first; hold the knot through the active frames (a climb hold that costs Strain, not position).
Anvil Fall (casts e3_cairnfall at the Peak's scale): Unparryable, E3, 1400 / 250 / 1600, 28%, radius 14 around the Peak's center. Mix-up: the sky darkens while you are on its back, at the center of the area. Counter: a climber drops off the far side; a flier lifts above 100 studs and pays the drain (11.10). Surviving it grants full Insight.
Drops: the rarest cuts (Hearty, Resonant, and Earth-touched, a unique tag), anvil stone (weapon material, top durability), leyline knot shard (the rarest glyph material), Tome fragment (earth family), Mana Crystals 6 to 10. Its table is shared with the Peak it guards (18.7).
Teaches: Pressure Tolerance; a Calamity Suffering source; the Rite solo Trial site (5.20); Conditioning for Fists at Mastered (the knot is the hardest soft target, with no Hardness).
Counterplay: CM: flight reaches the knot but the Pressure field punishes it, so the fight is an Efficiency exam. SM: climb or fly; the knot takes the blade well. VB, VF: a vowed character with Techniques climbs unpressured (11.3) and one without takes 50%; a vowed showcase at Master band (7.3).
Gloamwing (Master band; an Echelon 3 Pressure Peak). A moth the size of a chapel with wings of frost-dust, resting folded on a cold Peak; the dust it sheds is the snow. One per frozen region, fixed in the bones; a snow front follows wherever it has flown (11.7). It wakes when the Peak is entered, flies slow circuits above it and lands to attack: on the ground the fight is fair, in the air it casts. Caster; its hits apply Chill.
Frost Shards (e1_shard). Mix-up: from the air they fall steeply, so sidestepping means leaving a column. Counter: Block or clash.
Rime Wall (e2_rimewall). Mix-up: a wall between you and the only windbreak as the Gloaming starts. Counter: break it with an E2 or a Technique.
Wing Fall: Unparryable, E2, 750 / 200 / 1200 grounded, 14% plus Chill, ring 8 where it lands. Mix-up: it lands where you are; the shadow is the tell. Counter: Dash out; the grounded recovery is the only melee window.
Gloaming (casts e3_shardstorm): Unparryable, E3, 1500 / 400 / 1600, 22% plus Chill, radius 16 drifting with the wind. Mix-up: the storm drifts, so a safe spot stops being safe. Counter: the sky tell plus the wind (snow blows toward where it will drift). Surviving it grants full Insight.
Drops: frost-dust (Cold, the Cold-Front Broth's rare upgrade), wing glass (ice glyph material), Tome fragment (ice family), rarest cuts (Cold, Resonant), Mana Crystals 6 to 10.
Teaches: a Calamity Suffering source; Altitude Tolerance (chasing it near the 100-stud line); Pressure Tolerance at the Echelon 3 rating, where only mages at Capacity 800 and vowed characters at the Art threshold stand unpressured (vowed below it take 50%, mages below it are crushed, 11.3); the alternative Rite solo Trial site.
Counterplay: CM: a Grounding fight; an E2 knocks it down (4.10) into a slow ground target, and the storm is dodged by reading the wind. SM: Lances to ground it, the blade on the ground. VB: a thrown blade Technique grounds it; Spellcut the Shards. VF: Leap-Grab from a pillar; otherwise wait for Wing Fall.

15. Stealth and Perception
15.1 Mana Suppression (LOCKED)
Hides power level and spell intent. It emits a low-frequency hum (a heartbeat sound) if the observer is perceptive enough. Training: keeping suppression active near threats (Section 5.11).
15.2 Mana Vision (PROPOSED definition)
Mana Vision is the Sense ability that lets a player read auras. It is not on by default for new characters. Auras and threat colors are only visible through it. v1 referenced Mana Vision without defining it.
Sense (PROPOSED, v3): Sense is a Circuit attribute (5.4). It is unlocked by a mentor, a Tome, or by surviving an ambush from a suppressed foe, and trains by correctly reading auras, such as calling a hidden foe before it acts. Vowed physical characters cannot use it (Instinct replaces it, 7.3).
15.3 The Sense Gap Rule (LOCKED)
Suppression much greater than Sense: complete silence. The Master is undetectable to the Novice.
Suppression about equal to Sense: the audio hum is audible.
Sense greater than Suppression: the UI reveals the target's true threat level (color-coded aura).
Suppression hides what others see. It never changes the hidden Power Score (Section 5.7).

16. Controls and UI
16.1 PC Controls (LOCKED)
Movement: WASD.
Basic attack (Strike): Left Click (PROPOSED, v3; v2 had no primary attack key). Strike data by weapon: 4.13 (v3.3).
Dash or Step: Shift (directional).
Block/Parry: F.
Cast spells or Techniques: 1 to 5 for combat slots, 6 to 7 for utility (Flight, Suppression, Mana Vision or a cantrip, usable in combat; customizable, see 4.11; v3.3, was "non-combat utility").
Weapon toggle: R (Casting Stance or Sword Stance).
Cancel spell: Right Click or C.
Interact/Execute: E.
16.2 Mobile and Console (PROPOSED)
See Section 2.3. Mobile and console use the same fixed action set as PC (4.11): 5 combat slots, 2 utility slots and the shared actions, with loadout presets and contextual buttons. Console uses native gamepad mapping. No platform-specific parry windows.
16.3 HUD (LOCKED)
Minimalist: Health bar (orange), Mana bar (blue).
No levels: no numbers above heads.
Soul status: hidden. Check at a Seer.
Auras: only through Mana Vision.
Durability: no UI bar. Visual cues only (sword cracks, Section 7.2).
Strain: no bar. Read from breathing, posture and sound (3.4).
Progress: conveyed through visual spell forms and Seer hints, never raw numbers (Section 5.14).
16.4 New Player Experience (LOCKED)
There is no hand-holding. It is a hard, difficult game. (PROPOSED softening within that rule: visible landmarks, Anchor towns, mentors, and the Aether's first-visit mercy rift give new players something to follow without a tutorial.)
16.5 Survival Readout Without Numbers (LOCKED, v3.3)
Hunger, Thirst, Blood and Grey Health are read through the body first, then sound, and through style changes on the two existing bars, never through numbers or new HUD elements. Cue stages are fractions of each character's own capacity, so Constitution (3.5) shifts when they come, not what they mean.
Cue ladder (tune): at 50%, a first notice (Hunger: an occasional growl, a hand to the belly at idle; Thirst: dry lips, a swallow; Blood: slight pallor). At 30%, the penalty stage of 3.3 (Hunger: more frequent growls, a slumped idle, and the mana bar's refill shimmer slows with the -20% regen; Thirst: the LOCKED edge blur and a rasp in the breath; Blood: clear pallor and a slower Health regen pulse). At about 10%, critical (a gaunt face and slow idle; a strong vignette and coughing; Health regen stops and hands tremble). At 0: starvation brings a heartbeat and falling Blood, dehydration a heavy vignette and muffled hearing, and 0 Blood visibly drains the Health bar (3.3). When Hunger and Thirst rise above the Blood-restoring line (3.3), color returns to the face with a warm exhale. In combat, the 50% and 30% sounds are muted; posture and the critical and zero cues stay, so survival never competes with telegraphs (4.9). Every stage has a visual cue, so sound-off mobile play loses nothing. On login, the current stage's cue plays at once, inside 3.6's login grace.
Grey Health: Soul Damage (3.2) shows as a grey segment at the right end of the Health bar that Health cannot refill, because it lowers effective maximum Health until a secure-bed rest (17.4). It carries a hatched texture for colorblind safety.
Blood on the Health bar: Blood is not a bar. It shows in how the Health bar regenerates: a smooth pulse at normal Blood, slower as Blood falls (3.3), stopped below 10% with the frame darkening to a deep red edge that also changes width, and a visible drain at 0.
Take Stock: an out-of-combat action (PC: hold the inventory key; mobile: tap the character portrait in the inventory; console: hold View). The character pauses for 2 seconds and looks at their hands, and a short line without numbers names the state ("Your stomach is quiet. Your lips are dry. You have lost a lot of blood. Something in you is grey."). The inventory portrait is painted with the same state.
Haptics: console rumble, and HapticService on supported mobile devices, on the heartbeat stages (starvation, dehydration, 0 Blood).
Body captions (an accessibility setting, off by default): each cue line appears as a short caption as its stage begins, with no numbers.

17. Economy, Crafting, Inns and Starting Character
17.1 Currency (LOCKED)
Mana Crystals are dropped by mobs and bosses and used for repairs and purchases.
17.2 Item Dropping (LOCKED, ferrying rule PROPOSED)
Allowed: Glyphs, Weapons, Armor, and cooked food (PROPOSED, v3, 18.13), plus potions and salves (24.12) and Tome fragments, which bind when assembled (26.9) (PROPOSED, v3.2), plus Mana Crystals, materials, ingredients and Wellstones (LOCKED, v3.3). Crystals received by drop and by trade count together toward the per-receiver daily limit in 8.4. Dropped items despawn after 5 real minutes (tune) or when the server closes (17.10).
Not allowed: Spell Tomes and Manuals, which bind on pickup and never drop or trade (v3.3, was "soul-bound on use"; fragments are the tradable unit, 26.9), and Grimoires (character-bound).
Alt-ferrying rule (PROPOSED): economies are per slot, and drop and pickup between slots of the same account is blocked, so alts cannot funnel gear and crystals to a main.
17.3 Crafting (LOCKED)
Glyphs: created by enchanters using rare materials.
Weapons: forged by blacksmiths. Better materials give higher durability.
17.4 Inns and Respawn (LOCKED)
Initial spawn: players are assigned a random Inn on character creation (19.7). The assigned Inn is rent-free for the first 7 real days (tune), then normal rent applies (LOCKED, v3.3; it was undefined, and 5 starting crystals at 1 per day meant eviction on day 6).
Respawn: at your rented Inn (9.1, v3.3).
Inn rental: rent a different Inn for 1, 6 or 18 Mana Crystals (daily, weekly, monthly in real days, rolling from payment, paid upfront, 3.6).
Eviction: if rent expires unpaid, the player is evicted. The next respawn washes the player ashore on a random island (a Safe Zone, but far from civilization).
Reclaim: the player can re-rent a preferred Inn at any time.
Secure bed (v3.1 definition): a bed at a rented Inn, or at a guild hearth (18.12). Camps count only under the tended-hearth rule (18.12).
Using a secure bed (LOCKED, v3.3): out of combat (4.1), and not Wanted by the town that owns the Inn (12); the Inn stays protected (13), but a Wanted character cannot sleep there. Press E to lie down. Resting online heals Soul Damage (tune: fully grey to clear in about 3 real minutes), and logging out in the bed heals it fully. A secure bed also converts Body Load (5.19.2), builds Rested Circuit on logout (5.23), ends a Hollow episode (3.2), and restores suspended Vow perks (7.3). Guild-hearth bunks arrive with the Guild Hall (8.9), and leyline Claim Stone bunks count while a claim is held (8.9). Never a secure bed: anything in the Aether, an untended campfire, ferries and boats. A safe spot (5.23) allows meditation only.
17.5 Starting Character (LOCKED)
Mana Capacity: 100 (base).
Mana Crystals: 5.
Spells known: none. Mages and sword mages use melee weapons first.
Equipment: nothing, or a basic staff or sword.
Lives: 3.
Body attributes start Raw. Early melee lands in soft clay (5.4), so no starting habit locks a path (PROPOSED, v3).
Before Barrier (v3.1): until a character learns Barrier, F is a body Guard and Physical Parry paid in Strain (3.4, 4.12). Barrier is the first cantrip every Anchor trainer teaches (5.20), and learning it replaces the body Guard for non-vowed characters.
17.6 Monetization (LOCKED direction, details PROPOSED)
Sold: character slots (Section 19.5) and cosmetics.
Never sold: XP, boosts, breakthroughs, lives, or any combat power (Design Law 6).
Slots as gamepasses: permanent unlocks use a gamepass, which Roblox tracks. Ownership is checked server-side every time the menu loads.
17.7 Armor (LOCKED, v3.3)
One armor slot (a body set). Other armor pieces are cosmetic layers only. Class sets reduction and mobility cost; material sets maximum durability and weight (26.7). Values (tune):
Light: physical damage -6%, spell damage -3%, Blood guard 20%, no mobility cost.
Medium: physical -12%, spell -6%, Blood guard 35%; Dash and Step cost +10%, walk -4%, flight drain +10%.
Heavy: physical -18%, spell -9%, Blood guard 50%; Dash and Step cost +25%, walk -8%, flight drain +25%, and the wearer cannot swim (11.14).
Physical covers Strikes, Techniques, Arts and mob melee. Blood guard reduces the Blood lost from heavy hits and bleeding wounds (3.3); it never touches starvation, dehydration, Bleeding Effort (3.4), curses or Aether effects. Reduction applies before hit caps and combo scaling (4.11 Law 3), and also reduces block chip (4.5). Armor never touches a window, i-frames, dash cooldown, a startup floor, Reel or Guard Break thresholds (poise is Footwork, 7.3), or a Contest (4.11 Law 1). It pays in durability and mobility (1.2). An unreinforced Sword Mage blade at 0 mana treats the target's physical reduction as doubled (tune), which is the "bounces off armor" of 7.2.
Who wears what: anyone may wear any class. Mages pay for Heavy in dash mana and flight drain, so Light is their natural choice. The Bare Vow allows Light and Medium; fighting in Heavy armor is a Vow break (7.3), and equipping it out of combat is refused with a grimace ("Your hands refuse it"). Armor class is visible on the model (cloth and leather, mail and hide, plate).
Durability: every hit absorbed removes durability in proportion to the damage absorbed; Acid Rain and acid add wear (10.3, 10.6). Cues mirror 7.2, with no bar: above 50% none; 50% scuffs and dents; 25% splits and a loose rattle; 0% Broken, visibly torn, no protection but full mobility cost. An overworld death sets armor to Broken (9.1).
Repair: an Armorer at each Anchor town's smithy. Cost = class base x material multiplier x missing share. From Broken at base material: Light 2, Medium 4, Heavy 6 crystals (tune). Repair is instant and never raises maximum durability. Field repair is the Mend salve (24.4).
Power Score: armor counts by class and material at full durability, at most 25% of the Defense part (5.7). The reductions sit below one combo-scaling step, so armor extends a fight by about one read, never by a window. Balance armor through mobility cost before changing its reduction.
17.8 The Pack and Carry Weight (LOCKED, v3.3)
Equipped slots, which use no pack space: weapon, staff or off-hand, armor, waterskin, Wellstone. Pack: 24 slots (tune). Consumables, materials and ingredients stack (tune: 10 to 20; vials 5, 24.12); gear does not.
Weight is hidden on every item, and capacity is hidden. Might adds a little capacity (tune: +25% at Perfected, on the Law 2 curve). Bands (tune): Unburdened up to 60%, no effect; Laden 60 to 100%, walk -5% and slower swimming; Overladen 100 to 130%, no sprint, Dash and Step cost +20%, flight drain +20%, and you sink (11.14); nothing more can be picked up past 130%. Weight changes economy, never windows (4.11 Law 1).
A preserved ration weighs about 40% of the fresh food it replaces (tune), less with a ripe Larder (18.8, 18.11). Water weighs by fill level.
Readout: posture stoops when Laden and Overladen, the pack model grows, and the inventory screen draws straps tightening. The inventory and trade screens (not the HUD) show stack counts and the crystal count, which are counts of things, not stats (16.3).
17.9 Inn Chest, Larder and What Death Keeps (LOCKED, v3.3)
Inn chest: one per slot, 40 slots (tune), for durable goods (gear, materials, potions, Wellstones, crystals). It is reached at whichever Inn the slot rents, and moves instantly when you change Inn. On eviction it is locked, not lost, until the slot rents again. There is no paid chest expansion.
Larder (18.8): food, ingredients and reagents only, 20 slots (tune), at the rented Inn, with spoilage at half speed (tune). Eviction keeps the 3-day grace of 18.8, then it rots.
On an overworld death (or a killed combat-log body, 4.1): pack and equipped items are kept, armor goes Broken, weapons are unchanged (9.1), and the chest and larder are untouched.
On an Aether death (9.3): the pack, equipped items and the Inn chest are lost ("the dream keeps it"), except one Heirloom, marked ahead of time in the chest, which passes to the descendant at Broken (23, 25.7).
On an Aether escape: everything carried comes back except Aether matter, which crumbles at the Rift (18.7); Tome fragments and other non-edible Aether rewards survive.
No corpse looting: a kill takes nothing from the victim. Bounties are paid from crystals by the server (12).
17.10 Item Identity (LOCKED, v3.3)
Every non-stacking item has a GUID, and stacks carry a stack GUID. Every item records its last holder (account and slot) and its origin (26.17). A dropped item despawns after 5 real minutes (tune) or at server close. A different slot of the same account cannot pick it up (17.2); the owner can. The same check covers the guild larder (8.10) and trades (17.12).
17.11 Materials and Crafters (LOCKED, v3.3)
Launch materials (tune): Ironstone (common ore: basic weapons and armor); Deepsilver (Dead Zones only: high-durability weapons and armor, so the physical path's home turf yields its best steel); Leystone (leylines: glyph inscription, Wellstones); Hide (Mana Beasts: Light and Medium armor); Golem Core Shard (Golems' cores: Heavy plates, glyph binding); Slime gel (Slimes: potions, pickling, 18.7); Glyph Source (inscription stones in ruins, Fissures and events: required for every crafted glyph); Starmetal (Falling Star craters, 11.13: spirit-forged blades); Wisp (left by a killed Phantom or Wraith: quenching a spirit-forged blade; not edible, so 18.7's "Phantoms give nothing" stands).
Spirit-forged blade (7.3): a Blacksmith joins Starmetal and a Wisp. Only mages kill Phantoms easily (14), so a Blade must trade with or befriend one. The blade touches Phantoms and Wraiths at about 60% of steel's maximum durability (tune, 26.7), so a Blade carries two weapons and swaps out of combat through loadout presets (4.11).
Material sets maximum durability and weight, never damage (26.7).
Crafters: at launch, Blacksmiths, Armorers, Enchanters and Apothecaries are NPCs in Anchor towns. The player brings materials and crystals, and the result is immediate. Each Glyph Source speaks 2 glyph types, seeded per source (tune), and the player picks one at the Enchanter, so a wiki can list types but not which source holds which. Player crafting of weapons, armor and glyphs is post-launch, as a life skill outside Rigidity (5.26) following the cooking model (18.11). Player brewing is Section 24.
17.12 Trading (LOCKED, v3.3)
Who and where: two characters within 12 studs (tune), both out of combat (4.1), neither Wanted in that jurisdiction (12). Trading works in the Aether, where handing over rations matters (18.13).
Tradable: everything 17.2 allows. Never: Tomes and Manuals, Grimoires, anything bought with Robux (cosmetics are account-bound), the Heirloom mark, and Storied items (26.12). Another slot of the same account is refused, also through item history (17.10).
Flow: request, accept, add items, Lock, a 3-second review (tune), both Confirm, then the server swaps atomically. Any change unlocks both sides and restarts the review. One-sided gifts use the same window.
The window shows item visuals with durability cracks (7.2, 17.7) and the name line, plus stack counts and the crystal amount. A used sword is judged by looking at it.
Safety: no real-money link of any kind, so nothing tradable can be bought with Robux. No free text: the only user text that can appear is a dish name or a character name, both filtered (19.7). Anti-scam: the review restarts on any change; a warning shows if the other side changed something in the last 5 seconds; moving out of range or entering combat cancels. Requests are rate-limited (tune: 1 per 10s per target), and the block list blocks them.
Limits (8.4): crystals received by trade count toward the per-receiver daily limit, and gifted gear counts toward the receiver's Power Score (5.7).
Atomic swap: the server writes a trade ID and both item lists to a MemoryStore journal (TTL 10 minutes), swaps the items in both in-memory profiles, saves both at once, then clears the journal. On load, a GUID found on two profiles keeps the journal's or the newer copy, and the other is quarantined for review. Session locking (19.6) guards each profile on its own; the journal covers the pair.
Trade log: 30 days of trade records (who, which GUIDs, when) for moderation and laundering review (8.10). These are review signals, never auto-bans (4.4).
Resonance: a trade or gift raises Positive Resonance once per account pair per real day, sharing its cap with breaking bread (18.13). It feeds Sacrifice only, never rescue (8.1).

18. Cooking and the Hearth (PROPOSED)
"Mana is volition. So is a steady flame."
Cooking is the game's wind-down mode. It is what a mage does after a fight, between expeditions, and when the Aether has worn them thin. It is calm, but it is not a menu. It has tells, timing, commitment and mastery, the same grammar as combat at a tenth of the tempo and with no death at the bottom of a mistake.
This section replaces the single line in 3.3 ("The Cooking skill improves food quality and satiation") with a full system.

18.1 Intent and Design Laws
Cooking must be fun on its own, worth doing, and never mandatory.
It is a counterpoint to combat: slow, forgiving, sensory, and still deep. A novice can feed themselves in sixty seconds. A master runs three pots at a roaring flame and plates something people cross the map to taste.
It obeys the document's laws. Discoveries are recipes and tools, never stats. Progress is usage-based with variety gates. Nothing shows a number. Overworld ingredients are learnable; Aether matter is not. Power is never sold, and food is never power.
Food guardrail (LOCKED intent): food never touches a combat timing, a parry window, a mana cost, damage, Mana Capacity or in-combat regeneration. Its effects live in the survival chain and in out-of-combat recovery. A duel between a fed mage and a hungry one is decided by the players, not the stew.
Resting rule: time spent tending a hearth counts as resting for Recovery Training (3.1). The fire is where mages rest. This and well-fed Body rest (18.10) are the only ties between cooking and combat progression, and both are ties to rest, not to power.

18.2 The Hearth Loop: What the Player Does
A cook stands at a hearth (an Inn kitchen, a guild hearth or a campfire), with a pot or pan in front of them and ingredients in a larder or pack.
The verbs, in order of appearance to a new player:
Kindle: light the fire. A mage kindles with mana (a short hold). Anyone can kindle with flint and fuel. In Dead Zones only fuel works.
Add: drag an ingredient into the pot. Order matters (18.4).
Tend: stir, flip or baste on a tell. One tap at the right moment. This is the parry of cooking, with a window four to six times wider.
Stoke and damp: raise or lower the flame. Hold to blow on the embers, release on the breath tell. Tap a log to feed it. Pull a log to damp.
Taste: a free action that returns a qualitative line ("needs something sharp", "almost there", "the bottom is catching"). This is how the pot talks to you. It replaces every number.
Pull: take the dish off the heat. Pulling is the commitment. Too early is underdone, too late is scorched, and the window between is where skill lives.
Plate: name it, keep it, or hand it to someone.
A full simple dish takes 60 to 120 seconds (tune). A feast dish takes about 5 minutes. Nothing in the loop needs more than one thumb.
Why it is satisfying: every tell is sound and sight (a change in sizzle pitch, steam thinning, an edge browning, a bubble pattern breaking). Reading those tells is the same skill the game asks for everywhere, and hearing a sear land after a hard fight is meant to feel like a perfect parry that nobody could punish.

18.3 Flame, Tells and Tending (PROPOSED)
The flame is the cooking resource. It is not a bar. It is a fire the player can see and hear.
Flame bands: Ember, Low, Steady, High, Roaring. Fuel burns down over time, so the flame drifts downward unless fed. Stoking raises it one band; a well-timed stoke (release on the breath tell) raises it cleanly, a bad one spits sparks and raises it unevenly.
Every ingredient has a happy band. Cooking inside it moves the dish toward done. Cooking below it is slow and safe and caps quality at Good. Cooking above it moves faster, unlocks higher bands, and starts a scorch clock.
The risk dial: low and slow is always safe and never great. Roaring is fast, brilliant and unforgiving. This mirrors Exhaustion Training without the Trauma: the worst outcome of a Roaring mistake is a burnt dinner.
Tells: each stage of a dish emits a tell (visual plus a sound cue) a short lead before its window. Lead time is fixed (tune: about 1.5s). The tend window is generous (tune: about 1.2s at Steady, about 0.8s at Roaring). Windows never narrow below a readability floor, the same rule as 1.2 and 4.9.
Tend outcomes: on the tell is Caught (quality step up). Early is Nudged (no change). Late is Caught Late (scorch clock advances). Missing it entirely while the flame is high scorches the layer.
Sear: at Roaring, some ingredients offer a one-time Sear tell. Catching it is the only route to the Masterwork band. Missing it burns the surface. Sears are the perfect parry of the kitchen and are deliberately rare per dish.
Scorch is never instant. The pot smokes, the taste line warns, and the player has a few seconds to pull or damp. Every burnt dish traces to a decision (Fair Danger, 1.1).
Mana Zone flames: on a leyline a mana-kindled flame runs a band hotter and drifts upward instead of down (fast, volatile, prized for sears). In a Dead Zone only fuel burns, and fuel flames are slow and stable (the home of smokehouses and long braises). Sword cultures cook on wood. Mages cook on will. This extends 7.2 and 11.3.

18.4 Knife and Order (PROPOSED)
Prep is a short swipe game before the pot. Swipe across an ingredient to slice, chop with short taps, mince with a quick scrub. Cut size sets cook time: mince finishes fast and releases flavor early, chunks finish slow and hold texture.
The sequencing puzzle: a dish with four ingredients of different cook times is added in order, each with its own tell. The cook learns to put chunks in first, mince last, and fat before greens. Mixing sizes carelessly gives scorched bits and raw bits in the same pot.
Masterful play is parallel: two or three pots, each in sequence, at a high flame. Hearthkeeping (18.11) raises the pot count. It is the Parallel Casting of the kitchen, and it is optional.
Water and broths: boiling is the simplest dish and the most important. Boiled pond water is safe water (learnable). Boiled Aether water is still Aether water (unlearnable; it still drains Blood, 3.3). Teas and broths restore Thirst and are brewed with the same loop.

18.5 Quality Bands and Legibility (PROPOSED)
Five bands, never shown as a number: Burnt, Plain, Good, Fine, Masterwork.
Band is read from the plate: color, steam, a visible gloss, and the eater's animation (a Masterwork dish makes a character pause). The cook's taste line names the band in words.
Satiation scales with band (tune, in units of a 100-unit baseline stomach: raw 10, Plain 15, Good 25, Fine 35, Masterwork 40; Fine and Masterwork also grant a Meal effect, 18.10). Against 0.8 units per minute of play (3.6), a Fine meal lasts about 44 minutes, roughly three mobile sessions.
Burnt still feeds (tune: 8 units) and can never poison. Failure is cheap, so experimenting is cheap.
Masterwork requires a Sear, a correct sequence and full-band flame. It should take tens of hours of real variety to reach reliably, so it stays a thing people talk about.

18.6 Example Dishes (tune, illustrative)
Hunter's Pan (Awakening): one meat, fat, Steady flame, two tells (flip, pull). A new player's first cooked meal, learned from any Inn cook.
Marsh Stew (Apprentice): root chunks, then meat, then greens, Low to Steady, a long simmer with sparse tells. Satiation-heavy. Teaches sequencing.
Tidewheat Flat (seasonal): a grain found only while the lowlands are flooded (11.7). Dry pan, Roaring, a single Sear tell. High skill, short cook, tiny window of availability. The kind of dish that becomes a rumor.
Resonant Cut (Adept): meat from a Mana Beast (14) still carries absorbed mana. It must be seared on a leyline flame or the mana bleeds out as a bitter steam. Done right it grants the Resonant meal effect (18.10).
Cold-Front Broth (weather): snow-front ice, bones, bitter herb. Grants Warmth against a snow front. A chilled twin made with snow-front ice and mint grants Cooling for a heat wave. The dish that makes a front crossable.
Ash Loaf (Dead Zone): grain, fat, long Ember bake. Keeps for days. The expedition ration of sword cultures.
Aldric's Marsh Stew, third generation: a lineage dish (18.9). The same stew as above, named, with the ancestor's sequence recorded in the Grimoire.

18.7 Ingredients and Where They Come From (PROPOSED)
Every ingredient carries a few tags (Hearty, Sharp, Sweet, Bitter, Fat, Grain, Green, Bone, Resonant, Cold, Hot) and a happy band. Dishes are the product of tags, sequence and flame. There is no hidden recipe check.
Foraging (3.3, 5.8): plants, mushrooms, fruit, herbs. Identification is the Foraging stat's job. What a plant does in a pot is the Palate's job (18.11). Bleed Zone deceptive flora (11.5) is the main source of poisons, and it is learnable.
Hunting (14): Mana Beasts give Resonant meat that absorbs flavor and mana. Volatile Flora give acids and gels (a pickling agent). Golems give mineral salt and nothing edible. Phantoms give nothing, which tells players something. Guards and Marshals drop nothing edible, so the villain loop stays a non-farm (12).
Drift states (11.4): ingredients belong to states. Ocean states give fish and sea salt. Grassland gives game and grain. Ruin states give ancient stores (aged honey, sealed grain jars) that count as rare preserved ingredients. Some ingredients exist only in a 30-minute state. Rumors (11.8) mention them: "the tide left wheat in the lowlands this week."
Weather fronts (11.7): rain flushes mushrooms the next in-game morning. Snow fronts leave ice (a preservative). Heat waves sun-dry anything left on a rack. A Mana Storm leaves struck produce with a volatile Resonant tag.
Mana Zones (11.3): leyline-grown produce is potent and spoils fast. Dead Zone produce is bland and keeps. Pressure Peak beasts give the rarest cuts and are guarded by the beasts themselves.
Elemental fronts: mobs that arrive with a front carry that element in their meat (Cold, Hot). These are the ingredients that grant weather tolerance.
Aether (10): nothing edible leaves the Aether. Aether plants and water are traps when eaten, and they crumble to dust at the Rift. Dreams do not come back with you. This keeps Design Law 3 clean: no Aether ingredient can ever become a learnable recipe or a poison.
Purchase: Inn cooks sell staples (salt, fat, grain, water) for Mana Crystals at prices that never beat foraging. Convenience, not an economy.

18.8 Freshness, Preservation and the Smokehouse (PROPOSED)
Fresh ingredients turn. Three visible states: Fresh, Turning, Spoiled. Timing is per ingredient (tune, real time: greens turn in 6 hours and spoil in 12, meat in 24 and 48 hours, grain in a week). Spoiled ingredients cook only to Plain and can make a dish Turned (mild Hunger loss, never worse). Turning is a tell, not a surprise.
Environment changes the clock: cold weather and Dead Zones slow spoilage, leylines and heat waves speed it.
Preservation is a cooking sub-craft (the Larder attribute): salting, drying, smoking and pickling. Preserved food keeps for days, weighs less, and satiates a little less than fresh. Rations are what a player packs for an expedition and what a rescue party carries into the Aether.
The Smokehouse is asynchronous. Hang meat at an Inn or guild hearth, come back next session, collect rations (tune: 2 to 4 real hours to cure). It is the set-and-forget half of the system and fits a 15-minute mobile session.
Larder storage: each rented Inn gives the slot a private larder. Eviction (17.4) keeps the larder for a grace period (tune: 3 real days) and then it rots. Guild hearths have a shared larder under guild rules.

18.9 Recipes: Discovery, Improvisation and the Grimoire (PROPOSED)
There is no recipe book required. Any combination can be cooked. The result follows from tags, sequence and flame. Improvisation is the default, not a special mode.
Discovery: the first time a cook pulls a Fine or better dish from a new tag combination, the game names it a Discovery and records the combination and sequence as a recipe card. Recipes are knowledge (1.1). A card lets the cook see the sequence and tells in advance; it never cooks for them.
Mentor recipes: Inn cooks (NPC) teach base dishes and safe plants, the mentor role already given in 3.3. Player mentors (guild cooks) earn Renown (5.16) when a student makes their own Discovery, with the same delayed payout and caps, so recipes are not a boosting channel.
Rumor recipes: Inns and the Seer give time-limited hints about seasonal ingredients and the dishes they make. They expire with the Drift state, so wikis cannot hold them (11.8).
Family recipes: the Grimoire holds the lineage's Discoveries and the cook's Signature dish. A descendant who reads the Grimoire inherits the cards and the names (Aldric's Marsh Stew, third generation). They inherit the knowledge, not the hands. The Grimoire XP multiplier (9.3) applies to the Hearth attributes when cooking an ancestor's recipe, and catalysts are re-earned each generation, the same loophole fix as spells.
Signature dish: a cook with a ripe Palate may name one dish their Signature. It carries their name and lineage when eaten by anyone. No stats. Prestige only. Feeds the lineage ledger (9.3).

18.10 What Food Does (PROPOSED, caps LOCKED intent)
Survival chain (3.3): food restores Hunger, drinks restore Thirst, and Hunger and Thirst both above 70% restore Blood (3.3). Cooking raises satiation per ingredient and lets many ingredients become one light, slow-spoiling item. That is its primary value and it is enough on its own.
Food never restores Health or Mana directly. It never heals Soul Damage. Potions remain a separate system (3.2, Section 24).
Meal effects: a Fine or Masterwork dish grants one Meal effect for a duration (tune: 45 minutes of play, 3.6). Only one Meal effect exists at a time. A new meal replaces the old one. Meal effects never stack with each other, and where a potion covers the same category the stronger one applies and the other is wasted.
Effect list (tune, each capped at modest values):
Hearty: Hunger decays slower (tune: 25% slower). The most common effect and the baseline reward.
Hale: out-of-combat Blood regeneration is faster (tune: 25%). Blood regeneration still pauses in combat (3.3), so this cannot be cheesed. This is the effect physical builds will like most, and it is still small.
Warmth and Cooling: weather-front penalties (snow, heat wave) are reduced (tune: by half). Fronts stay dangerous for the unprepared, which is the point of fronts.
Clear: the Thirst vision blur begins later (tune: at 20% instead of 30%).
Resonant: out-of-combat mana regeneration is faster (tune: 10%). In combat it does nothing. In a Dead Zone it multiplies zero.
Soothing: no effect at launch. See the Soul Damage open item (18.18).
Strain (the physical path's hidden resource): untouched by food. It recovers fast and is hidden, so a food effect would be unreadable and a meta trap. OPEN, default no.
Anti-meta guardrails: every effect is out of combat or environmental, magnitudes sit under the noise floor of a duel, there is no effect for cast speed, parry, damage, density, capacity or in-combat regen, and there never will be. Cooks are never mandatory for PvP. The strongest reason to bring a cook on an expedition is weight, shelf life and a warm meal in the cold, not numbers.
Eating gives the eater no XP of any kind.
Well-fed rest: resting with Hunger and Thirst high raises Body Load conversion slightly (5.19.2), never the cap. This is a rest effect, not a Meal effect, and stacks with nothing.

18.11 The Cook's Progression (PROPOSED)
The Hearth attributes train by use, the same way spells do (5.3), with no levels and no numbers.
Palate: trained by tasting and by correctly reading tells. A trained Palate also learns a diner's cravings and aversions by watching them eat (3.5). Raises the specificity of taste lines, and reveals the tags of an unfamiliar ingredient when handled. The Palate also detects poison in food offered by others (18.14).
Hand: trained by prep. Faster, cleaner cuts, and the fine cuts that unlock textures.
Flame: trained by stoking, damping and catching Sears. Smoother flame control, longer stable Roaring, cleaner stokes.
Larder: trained by preserving and by rations that are actually eaten later. Longer keeping, lighter rations.
Hearthkeeping: trained by running more than one pot without scorching. Raises the pot count from one to two to three.
XP model: Cook XP = Action XP x Novelty x Familiarity x Attention x Audience. The structure mirrors 5.2 so the Seer can explain each term.
Action XP: a Caught tell, a clean stoke, a finished dish. Scorching a dish gives the Flame attribute a small amount (you learn from burning).
Novelty: a new tag combination, a new ingredient, a new hearth type or Mana Zone, or a new flame band gives full XP. The first dish of a kind is worth many repeats.
Familiarity: each repeat of the same dish cuts XP sharply (tune: 100%, 50%, 25%, then 10%), recovering slowly. Cooking a hundred of one stew trains nothing. Variety is the bottleneck here too (1.2).
Attention: tells caught pay. Dishes that were left on Low and waited out pay almost nothing. Idle cooking is safe for feeding and useless for training.
Audience: a small bonus when a player on another account eats the dish (tune: 1.25x), decaying per diner pair with Soul Resonance rules (8.1) and keyed to accounts, so alts give nothing after the first bite. It is a bonus, never a gate.
Ripeness and catalysts (5.5): each Hearth attribute ripens by practice and breaks through on a feat in a new context. Example catalysts (tune, two or three per gate):
Palate: name every tag of an unknown Drift-state ingredient by taste alone. Or detect a poisoned dish before eating it.
Flame: catch a Sear on a leyline flame during a Mana Storm. Or bring a Roaring flame back to Steady without a spark.
Larder: a ration you preserved is eaten by someone inside the Aether and they come back. A delayed payout, like Renown.
Hearthkeeping: finish three pots with no scorch at High or above.
Hand: prep a full feast with every cut at the right size on the first pass.
Breakthrough visuals: the cook's flame changes color at a Flame breakthrough. Dishes from a ripe Palate steam with a visible signature. No numbers anywhere.
Rigidity: Hearth attributes do not count toward Mana Circuit Rigidity (5.4) and do not suffer from it. Cooking is a craft of the hands, not the circuit. Nobody is punished for winding down, and nobody gains combat power from it. OPEN: a mild rigidity among the Hearth attributes themselves, so butchers and bakers diverge. Default: no at launch.
Seer lines: "Your hands know the knife, but your flame wanders." "You have cooked this too many times. Find a new root." "The fire was patient. You were not." The Seer reads the hands in qualitative bands, like the circuit (5.14).

18.12 Hearths: Inn Kitchens, Guild Hearths and Campfires (PROPOSED)
Inn kitchen: the default hearth. Shared burners (tune: 4 per Inn), a smokehouse, and the player's private larder. Safe Zone rules apply. Inn cooks mentor and sell staples.
Guild hearth: a buildable or claimable hearth with more burners, a larger smokehouse, a shared larder, and the Feast (18.13). A guild that controls a leyline (5.16) can set a leyline hearth, which is the best sear flame in the world. This gives guild territory a peaceful reason to matter.
Campfire: a consumable kit used anywhere in the overworld or the Aether. It is never a secure bed in the Aether. One pot. Fuel from the surroundings. Visible light and smoke at distance, so a campfire is a tell to other players and a draw to Mana Beasts that smell Resonant meat (a sign comes first, then the threat, per 11.4). The campfire is cozy and it is a decision.
Secure beds in the wild (11.8, OPEN): recommended that a camp counts as a secure bed only while its hearth is lit and tended, so the open item resolves through cooking. The fire makes the camp. Letting it burn down makes the bed ordinary. This is post-launch and keeps the PvP risk the recommendation asks for.
Hearth tales: NPC travelers may sit at a wild campfire and trade a rumor for a bowl. This is a second delivery channel for 11.8 rumors and a reason to cook in the field.
Cooking inside the Aether: campfires work, since they burn fuel brought from outside. Only food brought from outside is safe. The fire is light in the fog and the Horrors can see it too.

18.13 Feeding Others and the Economy (PROPOSED)
Handing food: cooked food is a droppable item (extend the 17.2 allowed list to include food). Per-slot economy rules apply unchanged, so a player's own slots cannot feed each other.
Breaking bread: eating a dish cooked by another account creates a small positive Soul Resonance between the two (8.1), capped per pair per day. Positive Resonance is what makes a stranger eligible for your Sacrifice Rift (10.12). Sharing a meal is how strangers become people you would die for. Meal Resonance never grants rescue eligibility (10.11). The gain is tiny and capped, so it cannot be farmed into anything else.
Expedition provisioning: rations are the practical reason a party wants a cook. A cook with a ripe Larder packs lighter and longer. Rescue parties (10.11) that carry rations can stay in an Aether longer. This is support that never touches a fight.
Kitchen Duty: cooking for an Inn pays rent in kind. A set number of Good or better dishes per day (tune: 6) covers that day's rent (1 Mana Crystal, 17.4). It never pays out beyond rent, so it is a sink relief, not a faucet. NPCs never buy food for crystals.
Player sales: players may trade food for crystals or goods as they trade anything else. Food is time-limited, so stockpiles rot.
Feast: a guild hearth may hold one Feast per day (tune). Several cooks run several pots in parallel on a shared timer. Everyone present who eats gets the Meal effect of the best dish they ate, still one effect, still capped. The Feast is a social event, a screenshot, and a reason for a guild to gather that is not a raid.
Reputation: a cook's name travels with their dishes. Inn cooks remark on a Signature they have heard of. There is no reputation stat, only a name people recognize.
Monetization: cosmetic hearth skins, pots, plating and campfire kits are sold. Ingredients, recipes, Meal effects and Hearth XP are never sold (1.2, 17.6).

18.14 Poison, Crime and Anti-Abuse (PROPOSED)
Poisons exist and are learnable. They come from Bleed Zone flora (11.5) and a few overworld plants, identified by Foraging. A poisoned dish looks like any other dish.
Nothing is force-fed. Eating is a choice, so every poisoning traces to a decision to eat a stranger's food (1.1).
Palate counter-play: a trained Palate sees "something bitter beneath" on a poisoned dish before eating. Detection improves with the attribute and is the catalyst in 18.11. Cooks are the natural counter to poisoners.
Poison effects mirror Aether fruit (3.3): they drain Hunger, Thirst, Blood or out-of-combat mana regen over time. They never deal instant damage, never cause Soul Damage, never take a Life directly, and never apply in combat. A victim always has time to respond.
Crime: every dish carries the cook's Residual Mana Signature (a Fist's dishes carry none, and guards trace them through witnesses and trackers, 7.3). A poisoning in a settled area is a crime read by guards exactly as any other (12). Heat accrues to the cook. Suppression hides a signature at the hearth as it does anywhere, giving villains the same tool and cost. In the wilds, poisoning is not a crime, the same rule as other PvP (23).
Griefing limits: a poisoned dish cannot be stacked with a Meal effect, so poison is never disguised as a buff. Dishes from a cook with active heat show as "a stranger's cooking" in settled areas.
Alts and boosting: Audience XP is account-keyed and decays like Resonance. Meal effects are modest and out of combat. Eating grants no XP. There is nothing to boost.
Economy: food has no NPC buy price, Kitchen Duty caps at rent, and ingredients come from the same spawn budgets as everything else (11.4). Cooking cannot print crystals.
Farming: Familiarity and Novelty make repeats worthless. There is no daily cap because none is needed.

18.15 Mobile, Console and Session Length (PROPOSED)
Touch: the pot fills the center of the screen. Tend is one large button that lights on a tell. Stoke is a hold on the fire. Cuts are swipes on a single ingredient card. Add is a drag. Nothing needs two thumbs at once below three pots.
Gamepad: A to tend, right trigger to stoke, stick flick to cut, bumpers to switch pots.
Legibility: tells are a sound and a large visual change, never a small icon. Colorblind-safe steam and gloss cues. Sizzle pitch is the primary tell and works with the screen in a pocket.
Session fit: a dish in one to two minutes, a ration batch in the Smokehouse across sessions, a Feast in about ten. A player who logs in for fifteen minutes can cook two things, hang meat, and leave with a Discovery.
Performance: other players see a generic cooking animation and smoke, never the simulation. Steam is a few sprites on mobile. Hearth audio is local.

18.16 Integration Points
See 1.1 and 1.2: discoveries as recipes, variety over volume, learnable versus unlearnable, power never sold.
See 3.1: hearth tending counts as resting for Recovery Training.
See 3.3: this section is the design behind the Cooking line in 3.3. Boiling makes unknown water safe in the overworld only. Aether water stays unsafe.
See 5.2 and 5.5: the Cook XP model and catalysts reuse the multiplier structure and the ripeness gates.
See 5.4: Hearth attributes are exempt from Mana Circuit Rigidity.
See 5.8: Foraging stays the identification stat. Palate is the pot stat.
See 5.16: cook mentors earn Renown by the same rules.
See 7.2 and 11.3: fuel flames versus mana flames mirror Dead Zone sword cultures versus leyline mages.
See 8.1 and 10.12: breaking bread creates capped positive Soul Resonance, which feeds Sacrifice eligibility.
See 9.3: family recipes and Signature dishes in the Grimoire, with the XP multiplier and re-earned catalysts.
See 10.9 and 10.10: nothing edible leaves the Aether. Rations are the supplies that 3.3 tells players to bring.
See 10.11: rescue parties provision with rations.
See 11.4, 11.7, 11.8: Drift states, fronts, and rumors supply seasonal ingredients and recipe hints. Secure beds in the wild resolve through tended hearths.
See 11.5: Bleed Zone flora is the poison source.
See 12: poisoning is a signature-traced crime in settled areas.
See 14: the bestiary is the meat table. Guards drop nothing.
See 17.2: food is added to the droppable list. Per-slot economy applies.
See 17.4: Kitchen Duty pays rent in kind. Larders live at the rented Inn.
See 17.6: hearth cosmetics are sold. Nothing else.

18.17 Technical Notes (PROPOSED)
Server authority: the server owns the cook session. It generates every tell with a server timestamp, records tend inputs with the same server-side input estimate as combat (4.4), applies the same latency cap, and decides Caught, Nudged or Late. Clients predict the flash and sound. Windows are wide enough that compensation is simple.
Tick rate: cook sessions update at a low rate (tune: 5 Hz). A tell lead of 1.5s and windows near 1s never need combat tick rates.
Caps: concurrent cook sessions per server (tune: 32), burners per hearth, campfires per region. Sessions idle out after a minute without input.
Determinism: a cook session is replayable from its seed and input log, like combat (22).
Data model (character profile, Hearth block): per-attribute XP and ripe flags, catalysts done, known recipe cards (combination hash plus sequence), a capped novelty set of combination hashes seen, a familiarity table with decay timestamps, the Signature dish, and the pot count. Target a few KB.
Ingredient item: ingredient id, tags, acquired timestamp, origin (region seed and zone type), freshness state computed lazily from timestamps.
Dish item: recipe hash, band, effect id, cook display name, cook account id (for crime traces and Resonance), created and expiry timestamps, and a server-only hidden effects list that includes poison. The client receives display fields only.
Grimoire: recipe cards and the Signature travel with the existing Grimoire record (9.3) and are character-bound like the rest of it.
Larder: a per-slot inventory keyed to the rented Inn, with a grace timer on eviction.
Exploit checklist additions (20.3): fire tend inputs faster than tells allow, send client-claimed bands or effects, eat a dish twice, race two sessions on one pot, and drop a dish between slots of one account.
Pipeline: tells and windows are data per ingredient and per stage, with animation markers like 4.2, so tuning never drifts from art.

18.18 Open Decisions and Recommended Defaults
Soul Damage and hot meals. Recommended: no change. Soul Damage heals only at a secure bed (3.2). OPEN for post-launch: a Soothing meal effect that shortens bed rest, never one that heals outside a bed.
Secure beds in the wild. Recommended: a camp is secure only while its hearth is tended, post-launch, at PvP risk.
Strain and food. Recommended: no interaction.
Rigidity among Hearth attributes. Recommended: none at launch.
Audience XP. Recommended: keep as a small account-keyed bonus. If data shows friend-feeding loops, remove it before touching caps.
Kitchen Duty rate. Recommended: 6 Good dishes per day covers one day's rent. Never exceeds rent.
Poison in the wilds. Recommended: follow the PvP crime rule (settled areas raise heat, wilds do not).
Aether ingredients. Recommended: none leave the Aether. Revisit only if the Aether needs a non-combat reward, and then as a cosmetic.
Feast cadence. Recommended: one per guild hearth per day.
Campfire aggro. Recommended: Resonant meat draws Mana Beasts after a sign (smell lines, distant howls).
Selling to NPCs. Recommended: never.

18.19 Minimum Viable Slice
Build order within the production plan (21.2): the cooking slice lands with the overworld slice, after the combat sandbox and data layer, since it needs Inns, survival and profiles.
Slice contents:
One Inn kitchen with two burners, one campfire kit.
Eight ingredients: two meats (one Resonant), three plants, water, salt, fat.
The flame model with three bands (Low, Steady, Roaring), stoke and damp.
One pot. Add, tend, taste, pull. Tells with a 1.5s lead and 1.2s window.
Five quality bands, satiation per band, and one Meal effect (Hearty).
Palate and Flame attributes with Novelty and Familiarity XP and one catalyst each.
Grimoire recipe cards and inheritance.
Mobile layout from day one, since mobile is 80% of sessions.
Deferred: Hand and cuts, Larder and the Smokehouse, Hearthkeeping and multi-pot, Feasts, guild hearths, poisons and crime, Kitchen Duty, campfire aggro, hearth tales, cosmetics.
Success test for the slice: a playtester who has just lost a duel sits down, cooks a Hunter's Pan, catches both tells, and says it felt good. If that sentence is false, the tells and audio need work before anything else is added.

19. Accounts, Slots, Places and Data
19.1 Places (PROPOSED)
Menu (start place): a lightweight place for character slots, character creation, settings and Continue. It is the only gate that loads characters.
Overworld: public servers with streaming.
Aether: reserved servers. A shared instance accepts newly fallen players for a fill window (tune: its first 20 minutes, up to 6 players) through an access-code pool held in MemoryStore. Alone instances are one per player. Rescue parties enter by access code (10.11). (v3.1: "one per player or party" made every instance Alone in practice.)
Dungeons are seamless and live inside the overworld place. The menu is a separate place so it loads fast (important on mobile), friend joins can be handled before the heavy world loads, and session locks are taken in one place. The cost is one extra teleport hop per launch, mitigated with a custom loading screen.
19.2 Menu Contents (PROPOSED)
Continue: loads the last-used slot.
Characters: the slot list (name, Inn, lineage generation, time played), with no stats shown.
Create character: assigns a random Inn (Section 17.4).
Settings: controls, mobile and console options, accessibility.
19.3 Joining a Friend (PROPOSED)
The menu reads the join's follow ID (set when a player joins through a friend).
It shows a slot picker with "Joining [Friend]". It never silently loads a slot, because permadeath makes the choice matter.
It looks up where the friend is. If they are in a public overworld server, it teleports to that server, with a fallback to any server if it is full.
If the friend is in the Aether: the lookup does not work for reserved servers (the lookup is unusable for them), so the follow fails by design. The player is sent to the normal overworld with a short message. Rescue is a separate in-game system (Section 10.11).
Roblox's behavior for friend joins into reserved servers has changed over time, so verify it on a published build.
19.4 Guards on Entry (PROPOSED)
The overworld does not trust that players came through the menu. Join links can land players in any place, and teleport data can be tampered with by clients. The menu server writes a short-lived ticket (for example in MemoryStore) that the overworld checks, and players without one are sent back to the menu.
The Aether lock and the combat lock beat everything: if the account is bound to an Aether instance or has an open combat window (Section 4.1), Continue and follow both route to that exact server.
Teleport failures: handle failed teleports by leaving the player in the menu with a clear message, and persist state before every teleport.
19.5 Character Slots (LOCKED)
One slot is free, and nine are paid (ten total).
A slot is a lineage (Section 9.2): Aether death gives a descendant in the same slot, never a lost slot.
Keyed to the account, not the character (PROPOSED): Soul Resonance, First Aether Visit Mercy, Trauma mercy playtime, and the Aether lock.
Per-slot economy (PROPOSED): items cannot move between slots of one account (Section 17.2).
Slot switching mid-fight counts as a disconnect for combat logging, and the account stays combat-locked to that server for the 30-second window (Section 4.1).
Staged release (OPEN, recommended): consider launching with fewer paid slots (three to five, in bundles). Adding slots later is easy. Taking them away is not.
19.6 Data Model (PROPOSED)
Account profile: slot count, purchased flags, account-level flags (first Aether visit used), total playtime, the Aether lock state, and the combat lock (server ID plus expiry).
Character profiles: one per slot, each session-locked while in use. Includes the Constitution block (four small fields plus an Acclimation value, 3.5).
Slot index: a small record the menu reads to list slots without loading full profiles. Full profiles load only on selection.
Soul Resonance: compact per-pair records keyed to accounts with expiry, within DataStore and MemoryStore limits.
Session locking from day one (a ProfileStore-style approach). Permadeath, lives and inheritance make a duplication or wipe bug catastrophic.
Rollback path: versioned backups, and migrations written as safe contract changes.
19.7 Character Creation (LOCKED, v3.3)
Appearance: a custom rig with fixed proportions. The player's Roblox avatar is not imported, because hitboxes must match visuals (4.11 Law 6) and body tells (3.4, 5.19.10) must read the same on everyone. Options: body frame (3 silhouettes inside one hitbox), skin tone, face preset, hair style and color, eye color, markings and scars, and a voice set (4 to 6 breath and grunt sets, since Strain is read partly by breath, 3.4). Height is a visual scale within plus or minus 5% (tune); the hitbox is fixed. A Barber in Anchor towns changes appearance for crystals, and premium styles are cosmetics (17.6).
Names: a first name plus a family name. The family name is chosen with the slot's first character and kept by descendants (9.2, 3.5). Each part is 2 to 14 letters, with at most one hyphen or apostrophe and no digits. A reserved list blocks NPC names, "Seer", "Admin", "Moderator" and the like. The server runs TextService:FilterStringAsync, then GetNonChatStringForBroadcastAsync, and rejects the name if the filtered result differs from the input. Persistent names shown to others (rumors, Epithets, the ledger, 5.22) are re-filtered when first shown on each server, and the result is cached; confirm current Roblox guidance on a published build. Guild names (8.5) and dish names (18.2) use the same pipeline. Names need not be unique. There are no paid renames.
Rolled on the server and never sent to the client: the Constitution block (3.5) and catalyst draw seeds (5.5).
Random Inn (17.4): drawn from starter-eligible Inns (Anchor towns; never an outer island or an Occupied town, 11.13), weighted toward Inns with the fewest recent assignments (tune) to spread new players. It is rent-free for 7 real days (17.4).
Starting kit (17.5): the player picks a basic staff, a basic sword or bare hands, which has no stat or Vow effect, plus 5 crystals, a full waterskin and 2 rations (tune). There is no class or Vow choice at creation (7, 7.3).
Descendants (9.2, 9.3, 5.21): after an Aether death, the player picks a new first name and keeps the family name. Appearance starts from the ancestor (family likeness) and is fully editable. Constitution inherits with drift (3.5). The descendant wakes at the slot's rented Inn, keeping any rent already paid; if rent has lapsed, they wake at a fresh random starter Inn with a new 7-day grace. The Grimoire is placed where they wake. Guild membership passes at Member rank (8.5). The Heirloom follows 17.9, and the starting kit is the same as a new character's.

20. Technical Architecture and Security
20.1 Netcode and Authority
Server-authoritative combat with client prediction (Section 4.4). Roblox has no built-in lag compensation, so delayed resolution is the compensation mechanism.
Custom projectiles: raycast or shapecast, not physics parts. Replication cost with many players casting is a real constraint.
Streaming: tune StreamingEnabled for the open world, seamless dungeons and mobile.
Aether load: an Alone instance is one server per player, so budget server cost accordingly.
20.2 Performance Caps (PROPOSED)
Caps on concurrent guards, destruction events, mobs per server, and VFX per scene, tuned for low-end mobile.
Server size (PROPOSED, v3.1): overworld servers hold 40 players (tune), with a few slots (tune: 4) reserved through the Server Fill setting for combat-locked and returning players, so the combat-lock rejoin (4.1) never meets a full server. Verify on a published build that a teleport to a specific server can use reserved slots. Fight sizes, siege scale, Inn burners and cook-session caps are tuned against this number.
20.3 Exploit Checklist for Verifier Agents
Have agents act as an exploiter and attempt each of these against a test build:
Call every RemoteEvent and RemoteFunction with malformed, out-of-order, oversized and flooded input.
Fire casts, parries and dashes faster than their data-defined timings allow.
Delay only the ping replies and measure whether the parry advantage grows.
Send client-claimed positions, hit results, mana values and timestamps, and check the server ignores them.
Cast or dash with insufficient mana, during Reel, or while dead.
Trigger Sacrifice, disconnects and server shutdowns during pending hits, and check that no duplicate or lost state results.
Race two sessions against one character profile.
Join the overworld without a menu ticket, or teleport mid-save, and check that characters never duplicate or reset.
20.4 Moderation
All in-game text is subject to Roblox moderation. UI names must avoid expletives (hence the Hollow state).

21. Production Plan and Tooling
21.1 Scope Reality
The full document is a multi-person, multi-year project. A skilled solo developer using AI tooling can plausibly reach a playable vertical slice in months. The hardest parts are combat feel and netcode, animation and VFX volume, the Aether's content combinatorics, permadeath data safety, and mobile input.
21.2 Build Order (PROPOSED)
Combat sandbox: mana, casting, block, parry, dash, delayed resolution, tested against a dummy with a latency harness. Numbers and starter spells: 4.13.
Sandbox presets (v3.3):
Mana Capacity 100, 120, 300 and 900.
Block Proficiency 1.5 and 3.0.
Combat Regen x1 and x2.
Dummies that stand, always block, always parry perfectly, parry with timing spread, spam Breakers, and spam Grabs.
A sandbox-only debug overlay (hit time, C, input estimate, window edges, mana drained) that never ships.
Data layer and menu: account and character profiles, session locking, the menu place, teleport tickets.
Overworld slice: a small map with Inns, death and lives, one Anchor town, and PvE training.
Progression core: one archetype, 4 to 5 spells, one mob type, usage-based XP with saving, and the XP multipliers.
Aether MVP: one layout (The Convergence), no mutations, a few Horrors built to the Section 4.9 standard.
The Drift and Bleed Zones: a few regions with premade states and spawn budgets.
World Events: Local events first, then two or three Regional types, then Great events (11.13).
Law: guards and heat.
Destruction: props and wards first, siege-scale last.
Everything else, added gradually.
Defer until the core loop is fun: Parallel Casting, Glyphs, the Sword Mage, most Aether layouts and mutations, full clash math, Scars, and consolidation.
21.3 Pipeline
Code: Roblox's built-in Studio MCP server (read structure, edit scripts, insert models, run code, control play mode) plus Rojo and Git. Connected clients can modify open places, so everything lives in version control.
Verification: skills that enforce smallest-change edits, end-to-end checks, tests that prove behavior, secure-systems review, and safe data migration. Give verifier agents a threat model (Section 20.3). Keep a human sign-off on exploit validation and permadeath data.
VFX: the most promising area for AI, because VFX in Roblox is mostly data (particles, beams, trails). It needs screenshot feedback to judge look.
Modeling: good for prototypes and props. Hero assets (Horrors, signature weapons) need a human artist pass.
Animation: AI drafts, human tunes. Telegraph timing is gameplay, so timings are tuned by hand. Build a small set of base animation pieces and vary speed, feints and combos in code to cut per-Horror animation volume.
Parallel sessions: fine for independent assets. Settle the spell data model and animation conventions first, and have one session own the core systems.
Art direction: write a style guide and reference kit first to avoid generic-looking output. Art direction lives in 27.9 and audio direction in 27.10 (v3.3), each with the deliverable due before production.
Pilot test: build one spell end to end (cast animation, projectile VFX, hit effect, parry animation timed against the 0.2s window) and time it honestly before relying on the pipeline.
21.4 Testing Limits
TeleportService does not work in Studio playtests, so teleports, friend joins and Aether entry must be tested on a published test experience. Plan agent verification accordingly.

22. Testing and Balance
Latency matrix: run every parry, dodge and block test at 0, 80, 150, 200 and 300ms, with jitter and packet loss, and confirm identical outcomes for identical inputs up to the cap.
Harness (LOCKED, v3.3):
Defender one-way delay 0, 80, 150, 200 and 300ms. Attacker delay 0 and 150ms; attacker lag must never change a defender's outcome.
Jitter (standard deviation) 0, 20 and 50ms. Packet loss 0, 1 and 5%.
A +400ms spike for 1s mid-fight, which must earn no extra compensation.
Scripted defender inputs at -20, 0, +100, +190, +199, +201 and +250ms from the hit time, for both parry and dash.
Attacks: Shard, Firebolt, Fireball (direct and splash), Torrent, Stonegrip and the unarmed Strike. 200 trials per cell.
It runs on a server-side delay queue on RemoteEvents, with Studio's network simulation as a smoke check.
Pass criteria:
Up to 200ms with no jitter: 100% identical to the 0ms baseline.
20ms jitter: at least 99% identical, and every mismatch within one step of a window edge.
50ms jitter or 5% loss: logged, not graded.
300ms: expected results come from the same resolver with C = 200, and the capping is logged.
Timing tests: assert that startup, active and recovery in data match animation markers, within one server step (v3.3).
TTK check (LOCKED, v3.3; 4.11 Law 3)
Assumptions, all worst case: a target at 100 Health that never defends; an attacker with unlimited mana; every hit at its cap (12, 22 or 35); Cast Speed at the floor; zero travel time; compounding combo scaling; one action at a time (no Parallel Casting). Time is measured from the first hit.
Cycles (recovery plus the next startup): Echelon 1 450ms, Strike 500ms, Echelon 2 850ms, Echelon 3 2400ms (at most two per pool).
Fastest kill, a pure Echelon 2 chain: 22, 41.8, 59.6, 75.7, then 90.1, which the 4s window clips to 90. The sixth hit lands at 4.25s for 103: pass.
A pure Echelon 1 chain needs 18 hits, 7.65s.
An exhaustive search over mixed sequences finds nothing faster. Rerun it as an automated test on the live spell data.
Under a flat reading of combo scaling, the 90% window alone holds the floor (also 4.25s).
Breakpoints: Echelon 2 recovery at 350ms gives exactly 4.0s, and Echelon 3 recovery under 1300ms loses the margin.
Group TTK is governed by 4.5, not by this floor.
Determinism: replay recorded input logs against the server and confirm identical results.
