Shape. Four large island regions on one streamed map (11.12), each about 2,000 by 2,000 studs, divided into 400 by 400 Drift cells and crossed by sea channels (11.14). Beyond the channels lie the Outer Shoals, the eviction islands (17.4). The Holdfast sits under a long overcast; the sky clears only for Calamity telegraphs and events (27.9). The four islands, clockwise: Longmere (leyline), Ashwick (Dead Zone), Thornfell (Pressure) and Lanternmarsh (thin ground). Sailors named it: a holdfast is what an anchor bites into, and the land is held fast by the people on it.
Template (11.11). Each region has one mana-zone identity, one school of thought, one bestiary threat, one survival challenge and one secret tied to the Aether. Each has at least one Anchor cell (town, Inn, Seer, trainers, reliable PvE), fixed bones (coast, mountains, leylines, dungeon doors, far landmarks) and 2 to 4 hand-built states per wild cell.
Landmarks. Each region has three fixed landmarks on its bones that read as silhouettes from anywhere in the region and as imposters from the sea (11.12 LOD): one lit (a tower or lamp), one tall (a stone or tree), one strange (a shine, a lid of cloud, lights over water). The lit one is always an Anchor, so a lost player can always walk toward a light and find a bed. None is on a map, because there is no map (11.8). Rumors refer to them by name, and Inn cooks teach the names in the first ten minutes ("the lamp on the cliff is Steelhall; walk toward it if you are lost").
Sea channels and ferries. The channels between islands are fixed bones. Each Anchor town has a ferry pier on the 11.14 loop, which sets the fare, timing and channel classes. A ferry is the in-world reason strangers end up sitting together, and a rumor source (27.7). Swimming and flight remain valid; flight over an Open channel is a mana puzzle (11.8) with no safe landing midway, which is the point.
One ruin kit, one people. Every ruin state in all four regions uses the same Held kit (11.4): the same black stone, the same socket shapes, the same sealed jars. Players learn to recognise Held work anywhere, which makes the Aether's Remnant layout (10.4) land as recognition rather than new noise.
Weather is regional character. Each island has a default weather lean that its fronts move against (11.7): Longmere rain and clear spells, Ashwick ash wind and cold, Thornfell thin cold and sudden cloud, Lanternmarsh fog and Mana Storms. A front out of character for an island (snow on the marsh, clear sky on Thornfell) is itself a tell that an event or a long season is coming (11.13).
Place names. Short, plain, English-rooted and sailor-given, named for what the place is or does (Appendix C). One short word, or two plain words joined. No apostrophes, no invented languages, no names that need a glossary. Faction names are plain nouns or participles. A word that names a mechanic is never reused for a place (so no "Still", "Grey", "Steady" or "Drift" in a place name). This keeps the tone quiet and keeps rumors readable at a glance on mobile.
Shards (11.4). The overworld runs on a fixed set of named shards (tune: 8). In-world, nobody explains this and nobody needs to; the Seer's one line on it is "there is more than one telling of the Holdfast, and you live in one". Shard names are plain sea words a ferry pilot would use, one word each, none naming a mechanic: Ebb, Neap, Slack, Swell, Chop, Lull, Fetch, Lee. They are shown in the menu's slot list (19.2) and spoken by ferry pilots and Inn rumors when it matters ("on Slack, the Weirs are flooded; here they are not"). Per-shard honours (first full clears, first-to-master Epithets, Occupied towns) are spoken as "on Slack", never "on this server".

27.3 The Four Regions (LOCKED, v3.3)
Longmere (leyline)
Mana zone: the Mereline, a leyline that runs the length of a long cold lake and out through its weirs into the sea. High density: fast regen, larger spells, the Holdfast's PvP ground (11.3). Its strength varies by Drift state, never its position.
Culture: the Kindled (27.6). Anchor town: Highwater, a stone town built across the weirs where the Mereline leaves the lake. Dueling on the weir platforms is lawful sport; law is otherwise light.
Threat: Mana Beasts. Beasts that drink the leyline go glassy-eyed and absorb spells (14.2). They supply Resonant meat (18.7), which must be seared on the leyline flame or it bleeds out as bitter steam (18.6).
Survival: thirst in a land of water. Live water (leyline water) burns the throat and poisons unboiled; drinkable springs are few and known. The novice's first lesson in Longmere is that a lake is not a drink. Leyline produce is potent and spoils fast (18.8), so nothing keeps.
Aether secret: the Mereline rises from a drowned Rift under the lake, a scar the Held never closed. In one Drift state the lake falls and a stair appears on the south shore (the Drowned Stair, 27.5). The Kindled know and do not say; the Seer says only that the lake "hums at the bottom".
Far landmarks: the Weir Towers of Highwater (two lit towers, visible across the lake); the Glassfield's shine on the eastern ridge; the Mereline's glow at night, a dim desaturated teal line under the water (never telegraph blue, 27.9).
Ashwick (Dead Zone)
Mana zone: the Ash, a Dead Zone covering most of the island (11.3). Zero regeneration. Mages carry Wellstones; vowed characters are at home (7.3). The Ash never drifts in extent, because land with no will in it has nothing to change with; its Drift states change dressing and reveals, never the deafness.
Culture: the Plainsteel (27.6). Anchor town: Wick, a low town of grey stone and smoke on the only living strip of coast, where Steelhall stands. The Steel Vow is sworn here and Blade masters teach here (5.19.6).
Threat: Golems and Constructs, the Drinking Stones and their wardens, which reflect spells and punish untrained fists (14.3). Phantoms appear in the Ash's fog states, half-manifest (14.1).
Survival: cold and hunger. Ash winds and cold fronts cross the Ash; Dead Zone produce is bland but keeps (18.8), so Ashwick eats Ash Loaf and smoked meat and walks far between meals. Mana-kindled fires do not light here; only fuel burns (18.3).
Aether secret: the Held drained Ashwick on purpose, through the Drinking Stones, to make one island the dream could never touch. They half succeeded. No Fissure has ever opened in the Ash, and none does at launch (revisit with World Events, 23). A Phantom driven into the Ash must half-manifest, because without ambient will to ride it has to borrow substance from itself (5.19.8, the Spirit Palm Long Road). At the Ash's heart is the Deaf Room (27.5), the one place in the Holdfast where the Seer cannot read anyone.
Far landmarks: the Drinking Stones, seven black pillars on the central plateau; Steelhall's single lamp on the sea cliff; the smoke of Wick.
Thornfell (Pressure)
Mana zone: Pressure. The island is a mountain, and its passes and terraces carry echelon ratings that rise toward the summit (11.3, 4.7). A mage below the rating is crushed: mana and Health drain fast. A vowed character is measured against the Body threshold instead (5.19.6, 11.3) and takes pressure at half rate below it; the Plainsteel say the mountain "weighs the hand, not the hum". Pressure Peak beasts and the rarest cuts live high (14.7, 18.7).
Culture: the Tally (27.6). Anchor town: Tollstead, a terraced city on the lower slopes, every lantern counted, every aura kept low. Tollstead has the Holdfast's strictest law and its best Readers and Marshals (27.7). Enchanters work here.
Threat: Phantoms and Wraiths, the Held's anchored dead, on the Wraith Terraces (14.4). Immune to steel and fists; the Tally hunt them with thrift, and Classic Mages learn to zone here.
Survival: altitude. Dry air and thin cold (thirst and cold fronts), flight that becomes lethal within a couple of hundred studs of the slope (11.10), and passes that close in a Great Front (11.13). The Tally's rule for the mountain: "carry water, carry nothing else".
Aether secret: the summit, the Pin, is a Pressure zone rated at the Rites: a Held Rite that was never finished, still pinning a Rift shut under the weight of its own certainty. It is the solo Rite Trial site (5.20). Nobody in Tollstead will say what is under the Pin; the Tally believe that unfinished will is the debt the whole Holdfast is paying.
Far landmarks: the Pin, a summit always under a lid of cloud shaped like a plate; the Counting Stair of Tollstead, a line of lanterns climbing the slope at night; the Quiet Gallery's mouth, a dark arch on the north face.
Lanternmarsh (thin ground)
Mana zone: none of the three 11.3 zones dominates. The marsh is the Holdfast's Mana Storm biome (10.6) and carries the most Bleed Zone states and Fissure sites of any region (11.5, 11.6). Its identity is thin ground. This is a deliberate bend of 11.11's one-zone template (Appendix B).
Culture: the Struck (27.6). Anchor town: Firmbank, a stilt town on the one firm bank, where the Inn's cook Pell teaches Marsh Stew (18.6). The Struck keep no hall; their Standing Stones are outside town.
Threat: Volatile Flora and Slimes (14.5), deceptive flora (the poison source, 18.14), and, in Bleed states and during an Aether Tide, lighter Horrors (11.13). Spellcaster mobs (14.6) are common here, which the Struck consider a mercy (5.12).
Survival: hunger and poison. Everything looks edible. Foraging is the marsh's real trade (3.3), and the flood season (a 1 to 2 week Drift state, 11.7) splits the lowland into islands and brings Tidewheat (18.6).
Aether secret: the lanterns. On some nights lights hang over the western fen (11.8). They are Fissures seen from a distance: thin places lit from the other side. At launch they lead only to Fissures, never to a Rift (23). The Struck believe someone in the dream is trying to find the way back. The Seer says the lights are "the dream looking out". Nobody who follows them without reading the grammar first comes to supper.
Far landmarks: Firmbank's stilt lamps; the Lantern Field's lights on fissure nights; a single dead tree, the Gallows Alder, on the central rise, visible from anywhere in the marsh and never drifting.
Scope cut. If the launch scope drops to three regions, Lanternmarsh goes: the Standing Stones move to Longmere's Drowned Stair cell, Pell moves to Highwater's kitchen, and the marsh rumors in 11.8 and 18.6 need a new home.

27.4 Example Drift Cells (LOCKED, v3.3)
Cell ids below are working labels (column letter, row number), not names.
Longmere cell C7, South Shore. Bones: the lake edge, a stone jetty, the ridge road. States: (a) reedbank grassland, the default, with game and grain; (b) low water: the lake falls, the Drowned Stair appears, and the cell becomes a Bleed Zone with a Fissure site at the stair foot, 12 hours to 2 days; (c) orchard ruin: a Held orchard rises through the reeds, with sealed grain jars, aged honey and a Golem warden, up to 2 weeks; (d) frozen: a cold front holds the shore, lake ice is walkable to the island in the lake, snow-front ice supplies Cold-Front Broth (18.6), and Mana Beasts cross the ice to the town's edge.
Ashwick cell G3, Ash Downs. Bones: the plateau edge and the Salt Road's cairns. States: (a) ash heath, the default, with sparse game and cairns to follow; (b) salt flat: after a rain state the ash washes to white salt, with a sea salt harvest, no cover, and the Marshals' hunting ground for the wanted; (c) construct yard: a Held yard rises with inert Golems in rows and one that is not inert, and a flat stone with two handprints worn into it (a Bare Vow shrine, 27.6), 1 to 3 days; (d) grey fog: visibility falls, Phantoms half-manifest and can be fought by hand, the Spirit Palm Long Road site (5.19.8), 30 minutes to a few hours.
Lanternmarsh cell L5, West Fen. Bones: the Gallows Alder on the rise and the causeway. States: (a) fen, the default, with forage and slimes; (b) flooded: the lowland is islands, Tidewheat on the flats for a Roaring-flame Sear (18.6), boats or flight between islands, and marked shallows that vowed characters wade; (c) thin ground: a Bleed Zone with the lanterns visible from Firmbank, a Fissure under the Alder, exploration pressure and deceptive flora, 12 hours to a day; (d) drought: cracked mud, a buried Held road surfaces and leads to a Tome fragment cache, wells run dry, and shallow-well Constitutions feel it first (3.5, 11.13).
Through-routes and parity (11.12). Every example state keeps its cell-edge entries walkable. Flooded and low-water states mark shallows and climbable stone for vowed characters. No state puts a hazard within 40 studs of a through-route or of Firmbank, Highwater, Wick or Tollstead.

27.5 Set Pieces (LOCKED, v3.3)
Fifteen handmade set pieces (11.11), each with its job in mechanics terms. They are candidate sites for the 11.13 events, not fixed homes; events are placed by their own framework.
The Weirs of Highwater (Longmere, Anchor). Stone weirs across the lake outlet with dueling platforms above the leyline. Lawful PvP on the platforms, the Kindled's hall, and the Seer's house on the centre pier. Resonant meat sears best here (18.3).
The Drowned Stair (Longmere, Drift reveal). A Held stair down into the lakebed, open only in the low-water state. A Bleed Zone and Fissure site at the bottom, Tome fragments on the landings, and a locked Boss Room door at the end with a tier 3 ward (11.9, 13). No launch content opens it: the Held stay fragments, and the Rift under the lake stays a mystery.
The Glassfield (Longmere, catalyst site). A hillside turned to glass by a Sunfall centuries ago. Lightning fronts ground here. Durability and Range catalysts are tied to the place ("win a Contest on the Glassfield under a storm"). A spellcaster mob that casts Echelon 2 fire lives in the glass.
Steelhall (Ashwick, Anchor). A dojo on a sea cliff at the edge of the Ash, one lamp always lit. The Steel Vow shrine, Blade masters, the trainer who reads bodies (5.19.10), and the smith who forges plain steel (27.7). The Ostrey family has kept the hall for generations; Ostrey is an NPC family name and never a player-lineage example.
The Drinking Stones (Ashwick, dungeon). Seven black pillars on the plateau ringed by Golem wardens with exposed cores, over a seamless dungeon (11.9). The place that drained the island. Might catalysts (breach a warded module with strikes alone, 5.19.4).
The Deaf Room (Ashwick, secret). A chamber at the Ash's heart where even Instinct and Mana Vision show nothing. Phantoms cannot enter; Fissures cannot open. The hidden Fist master of Ashwick is sometimes here (27.6). Nothing a mage needs is inside (11.12 parity).
The Salt Road (Ashwick, traversal). A Dead Zone pass across the Ash, marked by cairns, with vowed-only crawlspaces that cut the distance and a Wellstone-rationing problem for mages. Conditioning catalyst ("cross a Dead Zone on foot", 5.19.4). Ash winds in cold fronts.
Tollstead and the Counting Stair (Thornfell, Anchor). A terraced city climbing the lower slope, each terrace a lantern, each lantern counted at dusk by the lamplighter (27.7). Law hub: Readers, Marshals, the wanted board, the counting-house, the enchanters' row. The Seer sits at the top of the stair.
The Pin (Thornfell, endgame). The summit under its plate of cloud. Pressure rated at the Rites (4.7). The solo Rite Trial (5.20). From the last terrace the Rift's hum is audible through the rock. Flight above the terrace is lethal (11.10).
The Quiet Gallery (Thornfell, mentor site). A cave of echoes on the north face where the Tally teach Sense (15.2) by making students call hidden casters by their hum. Suppression and Sense catalysts, and a Split Focus teacher. Also where a wanted player can lose a Reader, since the echoes smear signatures (12).
The Wraith Terraces (Thornfell, bestiary). Abandoned Held terraces where the anchored dead walk, each terrace one Pressure echelon higher than the last. Classic Mage ground. A spirit-forged blade is tested here (7.3). A hidden Struck mentor sits on the third terrace, taking hits.
Firmbank (Lanternmarsh, Anchor). A stilt town on the firm bank. Pell's kitchen, the Foraging mentor, boats for the flood season, and the Seer in a hut over the water. The Inn's rent is often paid in Kitchen Duty here (18.13), because the marsh is poor in crystals and rich in food.
The Lantern Field (Lanternmarsh, the Aether's edge). The western fen where the lanterns hang. The densest Fissure cluster in the Holdfast and ground zero for the Aether Tide (11.13). A Fissure here is the recommended first look at Horror grammar before a first Aether visit (11.6): a failed run costs the rewards and a Hollow walk home, not a Life, so even a player at one Life can afford the lesson.
The Standing Stones (Lanternmarsh, faction). A ring of stones where Struck novices stand and let spellcaster mobs hit them on purpose, then sit and meditate on the wound (Learning by Suffering, 5.12). It supplies the varied sources Insight needs (11.5). A player can join, and a Struck mentor reads their Insight aloud in bands.
The Outer Shoals (sea, eviction). Three small islands beyond the channels where evicted players wash ashore (17.4). A Safe Zone with a cold spring, a wrecked ferry hull for shelter, and the shoal hermit (27.7), who will tell you where the nearest Inn is for a bowl of something hot.

27.6 Factions: Schools of Thought (LOCKED, v3.3)
The four schools are not armies and have no territory to fight over. They are ways of using will, each with a town that leans toward it and a claim about the Aether. Players never join a school in the mechanical sense: there is no faction stat, reputation number, map marker or HUD element. A school is who teaches you and what they say while they do it.
The Tally (efficiency and suppression; Tollstead, Thornfell). Creed: will is finite, and every drop spent without purpose is lost to the dream. The Aether is a debt: it grows on waste, and a loud aura is a confession. The Tally count, suppress, cast small and cast once. They train Efficiency, Suppression and Sense; their masters are flat lines in Mana Vision (5.11). They run Tollstead's law because reading signatures is a Tally skill, and they produce the Holdfast's best criminals for the same reason (12). Mentor style: makes you end an engagement under 10% mana before teaching anything (an Efficiency catalyst, 5.5). Saying: "Spend it like you will need it. You will."
The Kindled (burst and overcharge; Highwater, Longmere). Creed: will is a fire, and a fire kept small goes out. The Aether is a forge: it burns off what was never yours, and the Grimoire is the metal that comes back. The Kindled duel on the weirs, Overcharge early (6.2) and treat Sacrifice as the highest act a mage can perform (10.12). They train Durability, Cast Speed and Capacity by Exhaustion. Mentor style: teaches by Contest; you learn a spell's family by clashing with its next member (4.7). Saying: "Those who die for others burn brightest in memory." They are also the Holdfast's most frequent visitors to the Aether, and they know it.
The Struck (the hermit order of suffering; Firmbank and the Standing Stones, Lanternmarsh, with a mentor on the Wraith Terraces). Creed: pain is the only honest text. A spell understood from a book is a rumor; a spell understood from the wound is knowledge. The Aether is a teacher that does not repeat itself, and Fissures are its lessons offered gently. The Struck make Learning by Suffering (5.12) a rite of their own and sit in meditation on wounds. They favour Efficiency under duress and Block Proficiency, and they are the only school that treats first-visit survivors of the Aether as clergy. Mentor style: will not teach a spell they have not seen you take. Saying: "Read it on your skin."
The Plainsteel (the sword lineage; Wick and Steelhall, Ashwick). Creed: mana is a crutch, and a body that is enough never dreams. The Steel Vow (7.3) is sworn at Steelhall. They forge plain steel that does not go brittle at 0 mana (7.3) and teach the Blade's Techniques and Arts (5.19.6). Their doctrine is false by the mechanics: a vowed Blade who dies three times goes into the Aether like anyone else (9.1, 10.1). The Plainsteel answer: "Then he was not steel enough." This is intentional; schools are allowed to be wrong, and the game never confirms or denies any of them. Do not change the mechanics to match the lore. Mentor style: the trainer reads your body (5.19.10) and will not teach a Technique until your stance "makes no sound". Saying: "Every child of the Holdfast is a Blade until it isn't."
The hidden Fist (the Bare Vow; no town: Drift states, ruins, the Deaf Room). Not a school, and not named by themselves; outsiders say "the stonehanded". Creed, as far as anyone has heard it: the Aether is the world when no one is holding it, and a person who can stand unmoved in a Drift state is an anchor of one. They live in wild cells on purpose, to practise being the only fixed thing in a changing place. No Fist master teaches in an Anchor town (5.19.8); they are found through rumors and the Wandering Master event (11.13). Bare Vow shrines are flat stones with two handprints worn into them, authored into a few Drift states (27.4) and ruins. Reading the Blow (5.19.6) is their real teaching: a Fist learns from everyone who tries to kill it. The Unbroken Hand (5.19.8) is the only Fist title the Seer speaks, and when one exists on a shard, Inn rumors carry it.
The disagreement, in short:
Tally: the Aether is a debt fed by waste; spend less, hide more.
Kindled: a forge; burn bright, die well.
Struck: a teacher; get hit, sit with it.
Plainsteel: where will goes without a body; need less will.
Fist: the world unheld; be an anchor of one.
Seer: "not a where"; reads what it leaves.
Where the vows live. The Steel Vow: Steelhall, with a second shrine in Highwater for Sword Mages who turn fully to steel. The Bare Vow: hidden shrines only, never in a town. Renouncing either (7.3) is done before the Seer, who records it in the ledger without comment.

27.7 NPCs (LOCKED, v3.3)
Law (12). Guards are the town watch, scaled to the wanted player's archetype (12.1 to 12.3). In Tollstead they are Tally and read signatures; in Highwater they are Kindled and hit first; in Wick they are Blades; in Firmbank they are few, and the marsh itself is the deterrent.
Readers: Tally-trained guards who follow Residual Mana Signatures (12). A Reader has an audible tell when they find a trace (a soft count under the breath), which is the wanted player's cue to suppress or move.
Trackers: human hunters who follow footprints and witnesses, hired because vowed characters show only a Still Aura and leave no signature (7.3, 23). A Tracker is a Blade or a Fist and can be heard by Instinct (7.3). Vowed characters hear a distinct Instinct note for a Tracker, so the Still Aura trade-off is felt from both sides (if cheap).
Marshals (12.4): one per Anchor town, each built to counter the archetype most likely to be wanted there. Marshal Idren Holt of Tollstead (Tally, a Sense master who parries from sound). Marshal Brannoch Teague of Highwater (Kindled, Overcharges in the first exchange). Marshal Sera Ostrey of Wick (Plainsteel, a Spellcut specialist). Marshal Odo Reed of Firmbank (a Struck who takes the first hit on purpose).
Smiths (17.3). Garn Cole, Wick: plain steel, durability through material, never mana; he will not touch a mana-reinforced blade. Teodor Weir, Highwater: mana-reinforceable blades for Sword Mages (7.2). The starmetal smith (name withheld until found): the one smith who forges spirit-forged blades (7.3) from Falling Star starmetal (11.13, 17.11). Lives in a Drift state on Thornfell's north face and is found by rumor. Not an Anchor NPC, by design.
Enchanters (17.3). The enchanters' row in Tollstead. Glyphs always trade one stat for another (6.1), and the Tally's enchanters say so to every customer: "Nothing is free, so nothing is given." They appraise rare materials for free and charge crystals to inscribe; they buy nothing (26.12). A second, cheaper enchanter in Highwater makes Glyphs of Expansion only, because the Kindled like big.
Mentors (5.12, 5.19.6, 18.9). One per school in its Anchor town for Echelons 0 and 1 (free cantrips from Anchor trainers, 5.20; Barrier is always the first). Echelon 2 comes from a mentor who first makes you prove something, and Echelon 3 (a Calamity) only from a mentor with Renown (5.16), who is often a player. Inn cooks teach the Hunter's Pan and safe plants. Hidden mentors (Fist masters, the starmetal smith, the Struck on the Wraith Terraces, the Split Focus teacher in the Quiet Gallery) live in the world and move with it.
The trainer who reads bodies (5.19.10). One in each sword-culture town (Wick, and a Plainsteel expatriate in Highwater). Speaks in the Seer's qualitative bands, but about stance and breath: "Your stance settles. Your breath does not."
Rumor sources (11.8, 18.12). Inn cooks are the primary channel, near rumors first (5.23). Ferry pilots know which cells have changed since yesterday ("the south shore's dry; wasn't last week") and which shard they are sailing (27.2). Hearth-tale travelers sit at a wild campfire and trade a rumor for a bowl (18.12). The shoal hermit, a Spent mage who never joined the Seers, tells evicted players where the nearest Inn is and one true thing about the Aether per visit. Dockhands in Highwater and marsh children in Firmbank give cheap, unreliable rumors, sometimes right. Every rumor expires with the Drift state it refers to (11.8).
The lamplighter of Tollstead counts the lanterns at dusk, aloud. If the count is wrong, something has drifted on the mountain, and the line changes ("one short on the fourth terrace"). This is a diegetic Drift tell for Thornfell, with no HUD.
Names left alone. The Seer is the only NPC name taken from earlier sections. Aldric (18.6, 18.9) and the Varn line (3.5) are illustrative player lineages; no NPC, hall, dish or place uses either name, so they never collide with real player families. Pell teaches the base Marsh Stew; a player family's "Aldric's Marsh Stew, third generation" is their own named version (18.9). All NPC names in this section are disposable.

27.8 Seer Lines and Inn Rumors (LOCKED, v3.3)
Seer lines (short, second person, no numbers):
"Two cracks. The third is not a crack."
"Your circuit runs toward distance and thrift. You could fight that. You would lose slowly."
"Something is ripe. It wants a storm, or a height. It does not want this room."
"You spent will on nothing. Nothing is what you learned."
"Your grandmother broke through on a hill that is no longer there. The hill does not care. Do it elsewhere."
"The dream has had you once and let you go. It will remember the shape of you. Do not bring the same shape."
"I do not read dreams. I read what they leave."
"That was not luck. Luck does not bleed you for forty minutes."
"You are hungry again. You will always be hungry again. Your mother was the same."
"Three of your line have sat in that chair. One learned the Sunfall. None of them learned to leave a fight."
"Your hand is harder than your blow now. Good. Now your blow is the problem."
"You have been unreadable for a long while. Someone has noticed anyway. Trackers do not need to read."
First Visit Mercy (10.10) stays hidden. No lore mentions or hints at it; the line about the dream remembering your shape is spoken only after a return and names no mechanic.
Inn rumors (time-limited, cryptic, no markers):
"The tide left wheat in the lowmarsh. Dry pan, hot, one chance. Don't blink."
"Someone saw a man cooking on the Ash with no fire. No fire. Just a pot, and him looking at it."
"The Mereline's running high. Wolves down by the weirs have gone glassy. Good meat if you sear it where it fell."
"The old stair on the south shore is above water again. Wasn't last week. Won't be next."
"Holt posted paper on a woman with a flat aura. Walked out of the counting-house with the ledgers and nobody saw her glow."
"There's a hum under the third pass. Not wind. Wind doesn't keep time."
"Lanterns over the marsh again, west of Firmbank. Three nights running. Nobody who went looking came to supper."
"A Blade from Wick says she saw a stone with two hands worn into it, out on the Downs. Says she didn't touch it. Says."

27.9 Art Direction (LOCKED, v3.3)
Principle: readability is gameplay (1.2, 4.9). The world is built so that the telegraph language (4.13) is the most saturated thing on screen at every moment, on every device. 4.13 owns the telegraph palette; this section only keeps the world out of its way.
Palette ownership. The world owns the desaturated middle: wet stone greys, peat browns, grey-greens, bone, rust, cold blue-grey water, overcast skies. The four parry-class colours of 4.13 never appear in environment art at saturation (style-sheet values: white #FFFFFF, vermillion #D55E00, yellow #F0E442, blue #0072B2). Green is reserved for acid (10.13) and never used for a telegraph. Orange is the HUD's (16.3) and is kept dim in the environment.
World-side guards. Pure white belongs to the Parryable glint, so snow, salt-flat and bone surfaces (27.4) sit at or below about 85% luminance or carry a faint tint, and the overcast sky never reaches pure white. Lamps, hearths and candle light are amber-orange, warmer and darker than telegraph yellow, and the marsh lanterns (27.3) are a cold violet-white "that no fire makes", a hue no class uses. Leylines glow a dim desaturated teal, never telegraph blue. Rifts glow a warm pale gold, dimmer than telegraph yellow, and are the only non-hearth warm light in the Aether.
Telegraph shapes stay telegraphs. Shape and sound carry the class and colour reinforces (4.13). Environment art never imitates the four class shapes at the hand, on a weapon or as a ground decal: no hatched floor patterns, no cracked-ring sigils as decoration, no star-glint specular on props.
Element inside, class on the edge. The projectile body carries the element's tint: amber fire, pale ice-blue, teal water, violet-white lightning, ochre earth, grey wind, black-with-no-highlight void. The class marker lives on a separate rim layer at the hand, weapon or ground (4.13), so an element tint near a class hue never shares its layer. Fire sits near vermillion and ice near telegraph blue, so both keep a step away in luminance (fire darker and more orange, ice lighter and greyer), and lightning's violet-white body is a fill, never a four-point glint.
Echelon scale is fixed everywhere (4.7): Lesser spells hand-sized, Greater spells body-sized, Calamities sky-scale with a sky colour change. The overcast baseline exists so a Calamity can own the sky.
Silhouette first. Characters, Horrors and mobs must read as silhouettes at 720p with particles off. Strain is posture (3.4); tiers are stance (5.19.10); sword durability is cracks (7.2); Constitution is animation (3.5). No detail that carries gameplay may live only in a texture.
Aether palette. Same grammar, inverted temperature: the overworld is desaturated-warm, the Aether desaturated-cool. Hazard grammar is fixed (void edges have a mist lip, acid a green sheen, unstable ground cracks first, Rifts hum and glow warm pale gold, the only warm light in the dream because they are the thing you can answer).
Low-end mobile. Telegraph budgets and minimums are 4.13's (exempt from quality scaling and culling; 1 decal, 1 billboard, at most 12 particles; the class marker never under 24 pixels tall). The world's side of that bargain: dressing drops to 50% on mobile and gameplay objects never do (11.12); fog of any kind caps its density so a white star glint stays visible at the readability-floor distance; and no environment effect uses the telegraph priority group or more than its own budget near a fight.
Lighting. Overcast is the default on every island; direct sun is an event (Leyline Surge, a Great Front clearing) or a telegraph. Night exists, and lit landmarks (27.2) are the navigation.
Hand-set, not scattered. Set pieces (27.5) are hand-placed. Drift dressing is seeded scatter under rules (11.12), but every Anchor and set piece gets a human pass. Sparse beats busy; a room with three objects reads faster than one with thirty.
Avoid: neon, bloom-heavy auras, glowing eyes, chunky "Roblox fantasy" armour, floating runes as decoration. If a prop glows, it is a telegraph, a leyline, a Rift or a hearth, and nothing else.
Deliverable before art production (21.3): a style sheet with the four class colours, the seven element tints, and the overworld and Aether palettes as swatches with values; three silhouette sheets (mage, Blade, Fist); one Horror; and the pilot spell (21.3) built under these rules.

27.10 Audio Direction (LOCKED, v3.3)
Principle: sound is the first channel of feedback. Many readouts have no HUD element at all (Strain, suppression, durability, Drift tells, cooking), so they are sounds first and visuals second.
Telegraphs. Every telegraph has a sound, and class is timbre, not pitch. The class sounds and echelon layers are 4.13's: Parryable a single high bell "tink"; Breaker two beats, low then sharp, "thud-crack"; Unparryable one continuous rising whoosh across the whole startup; Grab a sharp reversed inhale and a short chain rattle; echelon layers from a puff and click at Echelon 0 to a horn or drone at Echelon 3. Pitch and colour of the sound are left free for the element. No environment or hearth sound may use a bell "tink", a two-beat thud-crack, a long rising whoosh or a chain rattle.
Phone-speaker rule. Every gameplay-critical cue survives a single small speaker: nothing below 150 Hz carries information (4.13), audible energy sits between about 200 Hz and 4 kHz, no sub-only cues, and no cue depends on stereo placement. Position comes from panning on PC and from a second, mono-safe layer on mobile. Telegraph sounds sit in 4.13's reserved priority group; the body cues (Strain breath, Winded, the exhaustion warning) are never voice-stolen either.
The body is audible. Strain is breathing (3.4): four breath tiers, from quiet to audible to nearby players. Winded is a specific gasp. A Practiced stance settles with a sound; a Mastered one makes none (5.19.10). Hardness on a Fist is a crack before it bites (5.19.8).
Suppression and Sense. Suppression is a low heartbeat hum audible only within the Sense Gap (15.3). A novice's suppression wobbles; a master's is flat (5.11). Instinct (7.3) is a single soft note at 30 studs from the direction of intent, with its own note for a Tracker (27.7).
Hums are information: the exhaustion warning before 0 mana (3.2), the breakthrough tell (5.14), meditation's circuit pitch (5.23), a Rift's knitting hum (10.13), a Drift cell's decay hum (11.4), the Pin's hum through rock (27.5). Each has its own interval so a trained ear tells them apart. None is a melody.
Cooking is the sizzle (18.2, 18.15). Sizzle pitch is the primary tend tell and must work with the screen in a pocket. Steam thinning, a bubble pattern breaking, an edge catching: each has a sound before it has a picture. Scorch warns audibly for seconds before it costs anything.
Mix hierarchy. Telegraphs over everything; body and hums over ambience; ambience over music; music ducks fully in combat. There is no combat music, because combat is read by ear. Anchor towns are quiet by design, so arriving home sounds like relief.
The Aether has no music and almost no ambience: the loudest things are the dreamer's breath, the Horrors and the Rift. Silence is the tell that something is about to change (10.13).
Diegetic where possible: an Inn fiddler, the lamplighter counting, a Reader's whispered count, ferry bells. Rumors are text (moderation, 20.4); NPCs make wordless sounds, not voiced lines.
Fixed library, varied data. A small set of base sounds per class and element, varied in code by echelon, speed and element rather than by per-Horror recordings, matching the animation plan in 21.3.
Deliverable before audio production: the four class sounds, the four breath tiers, the suppression hum and the exhaustion warning, tested on a phone speaker at arm's length and in a pocket.

27.11 Working Title (LOCKED, v3.3)
The working title is Moorings: where ships are held. It is plain and sailor-given, and it says what the Anchors do for the world. The archipelago keeps the name the Holdfast in-world; Holdfast is not the title, because it is already the title of an existing game. Fallback: Thrice (three Lives; short and ominous, but says less about the world). Considered and dropped: Driftbound (reads as a genre title), Wake (the first association is borrowed, and a single common word is unsearchable on the platform), Volition (Latinate) and The Held (opaque on a store page). A collision and trademark check on Moorings is open (23).

Appendix A: Inconsistencies Found in v1
Clash formula versus example. [Player Spell Mana x 1.25] - [Enemy Spell Mana] gives 0 for 800 into 1000, but the example said about 200. These are different designs (4.8).
Rigidity formula. It depended only on total stat points, so it did not make neglected stats pricier as the rationale claimed (5.4).
Grimoire percentages. A 500% base plus a 100% Sacrifice bonus does not equal the stated 700% total (Section 23).
Ping compensation wording. It added latency to the parry window. Replaced by delayed resolution (4.4).
Solo Instance versus Alone Mutation. The same mechanic was described twice (10% chance). Merged in 10.5.
Combat logging. A 15-second combat timeout existed, but no rule for disconnecting mid-fight (4.1).
Mana Vision. Referenced but not defined (15.2).
Block and parry on one key. Clarified: a tap in the window is a parry, holding past it is a block (4.3).
Expletive in the UI. The 0 mana state's working name would fail Roblox text moderation. Renamed Hollow (3.2).

Appendix B: Issues Found in v2 (and where v3 addresses them)
Echelons were names, not definitions. Only three tiers with example spells, no costs, thresholds, damage, telegraph floors or destruction rules; Grand Magic (now Rites) was listed as both Echelon 3 and a ritual endgame. Fixed by the nine-property table, Echelon 0 and the ritual tier (4.7).
One-tier clash gap undefined, and the 1.25 clash bonus had no name or owner. Resolved: gap rule in 4.8, the Last Word goes to the reader.
Clashing was pure arithmetic with no player decision after the cast. Replaced by the Contest: hold to feed, let go, or break off, with the lock point as a live readout (4.8).
Mana Allocation and clashing conflicted. Overcharging (6.2) gave +25% damage per +100% mana, but clash math used raw mana, so a big pool won clashes linearly. Clash Power now uses Effective Mana on the same curve (4.8).
Mana Parry at 0 mana. The parry "costs 0 mana", but Hollow mages cannot block. Clarified: Barrier is still a spell and cannot be cast while Hollow (4.6).
"Breaker" was a parry class with no definition. Defined, and Grab added (4.2).
Platform fairness gap. Mobile had 4 to 5 abilities while PC kept a 1 to 7 hotbar, contradicting "no platform-specific" fairness. Now 5 combat slots everywhere (2.3, 4.11).
No primary attack key. Starting characters fight with melee first (17.5), but PC controls had no basic attack. Left Click added (16.1).
Golem contradiction. Durability trains by "landing hits on Golems" (5.3), but Golems reflect spells (14). Resolved: hit exposed cores (14).
Dead Zones "suit sword-first cultures", but the only sword archetype, the Sword Mage, needs mana to reinforce its blade. The vowed physical path is the real Dead Zone archetype (7.2, 7.3).
Destruction skipped Echelon 2 (13). Fixed with all tiers.
Learning by Suffering had no echelon rule: a Calamity either taught itself in one hit or killed the learner first. Now it needs three survived hits from distinct sources (4.7, 5.20).
Flight had no counterplay for ground fighters. Grounding added (4.10).
No damage caps or time-to-kill floor, so a "meta build" or an Echelon 3 hit could end a fight from one mistake. Hit caps, combo scaling and a TTK floor added (4.11).
Aether generation had layouts and terrain names but no generation rules: no guarantees an escape is reachable, nothing about module kits, chunking for infinite layouts, timing, or version safety for persisted instances. Added (10.13).
Overworld construction was undefined: region size, what part of a region drifts, how states swap without expensive voxel writes, and how a player standing in a rich state forever is handled. Added (11.12).
Mana Pressure zones had no rule for who is crushed. Now rated by echelon (11.3).
Time units. The in-game day length was never defined, and Blood drains were written per in-game second, which would kill a starving player in under a minute. Soul Resonance reset on in-game hours, which would allow hourly XP farming. Resolved by the time model (3.6).
Physical characters had no rule for the 9.1 death penalty, which only names Mana Capacity progress. Now half of unconverted Body Load is lost (5.19.2, 5.25).
Progression issues (from the v3 progression review; fixes are in Section 5 unless noted)
Rigidity has no defined scope (5.4, 5.8). "OtherPoints" never says which stats share a pool. If the pool spans every spell, each new spell makes all training dearer, which punishes variety and breaks Design Law 4. The worked example names a "Defense" stat that does not exist.
"Impossible within a standard lifespan" means nothing (5.4). Characters do not age, and a careful one never dies. Rigidity raises costs but has no ceiling, so enough hours eventually buy Master of All.
The only release valves are drastic (5.4). They are death into a descendant, or one Aether survival. A player who misreads the meta at hour 40 is trapped for weeks, and the cheapest respec is deliberate permadeath.
Starting melee will lock in new mages (17.5). Every character starts with melee, and melee now trains Body attributes that count toward lock-in. Without a soft-clay rule, a new mage's first hours quietly push it into the Body domain.
Ripeness overflow is undefined (5.5). "About 25% flows through" does not say where it goes. If it banks into the next tier, players can farm past a gate after all.
Catalysts can be solved on a wiki (5.5). The examples are fixed feats anyone can list, so gates become a checklist. 5.5 says catalysts force exploration, but none of the examples needs a place.
The anti-carry catalyst rule is vague (5.5). "No stronger ally nearby" has no radius or timing, and says nothing about heals, buffs or barriers. Read literally, it also blocks a friend escorting you, which gives safety, not progress.
Lives and breakthroughs have no budget (5.1, 5.5, 9.1). Majors restore Lives, but nothing says how often Majors happen. After build complete Majors dry up, so the most invested characters can no longer restore Lives and become the most fragile.
Lives after an Aether escape are undefined (9.1, 10.10). A character enters the Aether at zero Lives. If it escapes with zero, its next overworld death sends it straight back to the Aether.
The late game has goals but no progression (5.16). Once stats soft-cap and Rigidity bites, nothing changes how a Master plays after month one. Leylines and relics are activities, not growth.
The pacing bands contradict the month target (5.15). Master starts at 150 hours, which is 5 hours a day for 30 days. "Dedicated" and "build complete" are never defined, so Base XP cannot be solved for.
A 15-minute mobile session can come up empty (2.2, 5.5). Once attributes are ripe, play pays 25% until a catalyst turns up. Familiarity and the Drift push players to travel, and travel eats a short session. The Seer is the only way to read progress, and the Seer is a trip away.
Exhaustion Training has no risk (3.1, 5.9). Out-of-combat regen is rapid, so a player can drain mana into the air, rest and repeat. Stopping at 1% avoids Mana Burn entirely.
The Power Score ignores Body, gear and timing (5.7). A Fist master would read as weak, so he farms inflated Threat on everyone and mages farm him. Gear is not counted, so gifted glyphs are free power. A player who unequips gear or starves before a fight could lower the score to inflate Threat.
Grouping is strictly worse for progress (5.2, 5.6, 8.3). Split budgets, the strongest-member Threat rule and the catalyst rule all penalize groups. Nothing in progression rewards a guild, so the Social Ecology pillar rests only on rescue and leylines.
Risk-weighted contribution punishes the Sniper (5.6). Rigidity endorses a ranged specialist, but contribution pays mostly for being attacked. A Range-specialized mage earns less in every group fight.
The descendant's experience is unspecified (9.3). Nothing says what happens to Capacity, Body, Lives, Rigidity, items or recipes on inheritance. The multiplier covers only mastered spells. Re-earning every catalyst each generation, with no help, turns a lineage into a chore list.
Several named stats have no training rule (3.1, 4.5, 15.2). Combat Regen, Block Proficiency and Sense are referenced but missing from 5.3. Sense (Mana Vision) has no way to acquire it at all.
"Levels" leaks into rules (5.18, 6.2). "Cap dummy XP at Apprentice levels" and "At high levels" use a concept the game does not have.
Echelon 3 by Suffering is near-impossible (5.12). Insight scales with damage taken, and a Calamity usually kills the mage it hits. Some spells may also have no PvE source, which breaks PvE parity.
Mana Allocation can break the new echelon bands (6.2). Dumping +100% mana adds power, and that power must stay inside the spell's band.
Strain contradicts two lines (1.1, and 4.10, which was 4.8 in v2). "There is no stamina bar" and "Dash is not a stamina action" both need restating for physical characters.
Sacrifice is mage-only (10.12). It needs 500 Capacity, and a full physical has a small pool. Physical lineages lose the game's most heroic death and its Grimoire bonus.
Life skills tax combat (5.8). Foraging counts toward Rigidity. With Cooking coming, a character who cooks or forages would become a weaker fighter.
Verification pass (v3.1; an independent review of v3, every item fixed in the section named)
Aether reroll: a server shutdown rolled a new instance, and Alone servers close on every logout (10.8).
Shared Aether instances had no way to receive players; 19.1 gave each player its own server (19.1).
Rescue griefing: one shared meal made a stranger eligible to enter a new player's Aether and kill them (10.11, 18.13).
The TTK floor did not hold: capped hits ignored combo scaling, and a five-hit chain dealt 103% in about 3 seconds (4.11).
Body rest every 4 hours allowed about 9 hours of conversion a day, breaking the anti-marathon claim and the Fist calendar floor (5.19.2).
Carrying without damage: clashes, interrupts, control and aggro were outside the Unaided rule and the party definition (5.2, 5.5).
One tag from a passer-by zeroed a new player's Threat (5.2).
Logging out during an attacker's wind-up or a pursuit was a safe save (4.1).
The Echelon 3 Suffering route could be handed out by friends, since the hit cap made every hit survivable (5.20).
Account keying was treated as stopping alts, which are separate accounts (8.4).
Positive Resonance and "a fight" were undefined (8.1).
Spellcut had no echelon, so the gap rule forbade cutting Echelon 2 (4.12, 5.19.8).
Drift occupancy broke "computed from the clock alone" (11.4), and per-server world state could not outlive Roblox servers (world shards, 11.4).
The Major breakthrough list could not meet its own Lives budget (5.25).
Aether entry state was undefined, and the drink arithmetic was wrong (3.6).
Chained Steps cost no net Strain (3.4).
Alone instances could draw mobs a vowed character cannot damage (10.7).
A new character had no action on F before learning Barrier (17.5).
Trauma had a duration but no effect (3.2).
Utility slots, Flight and Contest feeding were PC-only or overloaded (4.8, 4.11, 16.2).
No server size was stated, and a full server blocked the combat-lock rejoin (20.2).
The Power Score freeze and durability rule contradicted each other (5.7).
A Fist could equip a weapon out of combat without breaking the Bare Vow (7.3).
Two catalyst examples were impossible under the rules (5.19.4, 5.19.8).
Mana Burn's trigger was contradictory (3.1), cantrip inheritance contradicted the Honed rule (5.21), Outpouring was undefined (13), Body thresholds had no values (5.19.6, 11.3), and the rescuer's fate was undefined (10.11).
Small fixes: 8.4, 5.19.5, 5.19.6, 13 and 18.12 wording; hunger now slows Strain recovery (3.3); the Resonance timer is capped (8.1); Fissure failure no longer costs a Life (11.6); the Contest margin is 35% (4.8); flight ceilings are in studs (11.10); Trauma depth and secure beds are defined (3.2, 17.4).
Still open (flagged for decision)
In-combat mana regeneration (0.1% per 5 seconds) is so small that the 10-second stalling halt (3.1) has almost no effect. Either raise the trickle so the halt matters, or drop the halt rule for simplicity. RESOLVED in v3.3 (3.1): the trickle is 1% per 5 seconds, the halt stays, and "passive" is defined.
Bestiary merge (v3.3; contradictions the bestiary resolved, each fixed in the section named)
An Echelon 2 Grab had two startup floors, 400 (4.12) and 450 (4.7): the higher applies (4.12).
The readability floor was "tune per attack type" beside the echelon startup floors: the echelon floor is the minimum, and one attack may sit higher, never lower (4.9).
The speed multiplier never met the floor, and scaling recovery would have moved Horror time-to-kill below the TTK floor on some servers: startup is authored at 1.3x the floor and clamped, and the roll never touches recovery or active frames (4.9).
Horrors have "no weaknesses" (10.1), but nothing ruled out the role immunities of Section 14 on them: Horrors carry no immunities or resistances of any kind (10.14, 14.1).
Absorb, Reflect and the Phantom half-manifest (5.19.8) were named but undefined: Glut, Vent and Emptied; same-spell return at 60% energy; 50% physical damage and no Phase-step in Dead Zones (14.1).
Guards cast table spells, which made crime a spell-learning loop against 12's no-farm rule: guard Insight is 25%, the Marshal's Verdict full (12.1).
Creatures needed spells the table lacked: seven creature spells added (4.13), and no creature casts a creature-only spell (14.6).
The element buffs of 10.5 (slow, burn, mana drain) had no status definitions: Soaked, Singe, mana drain, Stung and Root join Chill, additive to hit reactions and never reduced by food (4.13).
Rain's "weaker fire spells" (10.5) did not say whose: the player's (10.14).
Grounding (4.10) named only Echelon 2 spells and Techniques, so a Fist's Leap-Grab could not ground a flier: Grab-class Techniques and spells now ground (4.10).
A single hit's knockback could carry a player over a lethal edge: displacement stops at the mist lip (4.11 Law 3).
World and lore merge (v3.3; doc conflicts the world proposal raised, each settled in the section named)
Ferries were free in the proposal; 11.14's fare, timings and channel classes stand (27.2).
The enchanters' row bought rare materials for crystals, against 26.12's no-NPC-buys rule: they appraise free, charge to inscribe and buy nothing (26.12, 27.7).
The rescue lore let only fighting companions follow you in; guildmates with the 72-hour tenure also qualify (10.11, 27.1).
Lanternmarsh has none of the three 11.3 zone types: its identity is a Mana Storm biome plus the most Bleed Zone and Fissure states, a deliberate bend of 11.11's one-zone template (27.3). Fallback if rejected: a weak tributary of the Mereline, with Bleed Zones as flavour.
Plainsteel doctrine says a body that is enough never dreams, but vowed characters die into the Aether like anyone (9.1, 10.1). Deliberate: schools are allowed to be wrong; do not change the mechanics to match (27.6).
The Seers' origin implies a permanent outcome of a terminal Mana Burn, which 3.2 treats as Trauma: lore only, never a player state, and not a Scars rule (27.1).
11.12 allows 3 to 4 regions; the Holdfast has four, and a cut to three drops Lanternmarsh (27.3).
Dueling on the Weirs of Highwater is lawful sport, a named exception to 12's settled-area crime rule (27.3, 27.5).
Name collisions the proposal introduced were cleared by the owner in v3.3 (Appendix C).

Appendix C: Naming Pass (v3.1)
Naming rules, so new names stay in one register
Plain words a person in the world would actually say: a cook, a smith, a soldier. Names describe what a thing is or does, not how impressive it is.
Prefer one short word, or two plain words joined (Spellcut, Wellstone, Mirrorfog, Deadcalm). Avoid "Ultimate", "Supreme", "Abyss", "Destroyer" and other hype words.
No borrowed names from other games or tabletop systems, and no tech words in the fiction (battery, dump, null, bonus).
One name, one meaning. A word that names a mechanic is not reused for a different mechanic (v3 had Steady, Rested, Strain, Legacy, Tempered and four clocks each meaning two things).
Design-only terms (Skill Over Build, Power Score, Clash Power, Peer Band) can stay functional, since players never see them.
Renames (old: new, reason)
Answer Bonus: Last Word. The spell cast second gets the last word. A bonus is a UI word.
Answer assist: Intercept assist. Describes what it does.
Echelon 1 Light, Echelon 2 Medium, Echelon 3 Ultimates: Lesser, Greater, Calamities. "Light" collided with light magic, "Ultimate" is a hype word, and a meteor is a calamity.
Grand Magic: Rites. A ritual tier deserves a ritual word.
Vow of Steel: the Steel Vow. Vow of the Open Hand: the Bare Vow. "Open Hand" is borrowed from a tabletop monk subclass.
Null Aura: Still Aura. "Null" is a programming word.
Mountain Breaker (Fist Art): Felling Blow. It also clashed with the Breaker parry class.
Tempered (Fist Conditioning gate): Calloused. Tempered collided with Tempering (5.22).
Bloodline Echo and Bloodline bonus: Blood Memory, one name for both. "Echoes" is another game's currency.
Legacy (mentor prestige): Renown. Legacy already names inheritance (9.3).
Circuit strain (5.20): Reaching. Strain is the physical resource (3.4).
Concord catalysts: Company catalysts. A company is a group that travels together.
Four clocks (3.6): Four kinds of time. 5.1 already uses four clocks for progression speed.
Fresh Spawn: newcomer. Spawn is a server word.
Mana Battery: Wellstone. Mana Dump: Outpouring. Mana echo (Drift aftermath): mana stain. The fiction has no batteries, and dump is crude.
The Aether World (The Depths): The Aether World. "The Depths" is another game's realm.
Aether layouts: The Layered Depths to The Strata; The Infinite Depths to The Deep Strata; The Overgrown Void to The Hanging Garden; The Broken Civilization to The Remnant; The Endless Jungle to The Endless Green; The Acid Abyss to The Weeping Dark; The Evolving Fog to The Mirrorfog. The Convergence and The Grey Marble keep their names.
World events: Mana Eclipse to Deadcalm; The Sundering to The Parting.
Meal effects: Steady (Blood) to Hale, because Steady is a flame band; Rested to Soothing, because Rested names the Rested Circuit.
Spark (Echelon 0 fire cantrip, v3.3): Cinder. "Spark" already names the neutral contact spark (4.4), and Ember is a flame band (18.3).
Hold drain (Barrier): Block drain. Hold names the spell Hold (4.11).
Barrier exhaustion: Barrier collapse. Exhaustion names Mana Exhaustion (3.2).
Phantom Echo (spirit-forge material): Wisp. "Echoes" is another game's currency.
Volatile Gel: Slime gel. Volatiles names the thrown flasks (24.4).
Nexus stone (leyline claim): Claim Stone. Nexus is borrowed from another game.
Glyph of Haste: Glyph of Quick. Haste is a tabletop spell name.
Glyph of Reach: Glyph of Distance. Reach is a technique attribute (5.19.5).
Glyph of Density: Glyph of Force. Density is the Durability attribute (5.3).
Glyph of Weight: Glyph of Heft. Weight is a technique attribute (5.19.5).
Glyph of Breath: Glyph of Ease. Breath is a Body attribute (5.4).
Rivalry and Bond (proposal terms): Soul Resonance and Positive Resonance, the names 8.1 already uses.
Alchemist NPC: Apothecary (24.19).
Bestiary creatures (v3.3): Latch Automaton: Latch Crab. "Automaton" is a tech word, and a crab is what it is. Echo of the Drowned: the Drowned. "Echo" is another game's currency. Choir of Hands: the Choir. Pilgrim of the Shallows: the Wader. Three words each; one plain word carries it, and a wader walks in shallows.
Stillborn Colossus: the Unformed. "Stillborn" is an edgy word and "Colossus" a hype word; it is a giant that never finished forming. The Understudy: the Likeness. A stage word for a Horror that copies you; a likeness is the plain word for a copy of a person.
Bestiary actions (v3.3): Swallow the Room: Swallow. Lunge from Nothing: Blind Lunge. Sweep to the Edge: Edge Sweep. Shorter, plain words. Fall to Knees: Collapse. Three words, and it collided with the Unburied's Kneel. Baptism: Carry. A loaded word for what is a carry. Font: Splash. Font reads as a typeface.
Echo Bolt, Echo Lance: Mirror Bolt, Mirror Lance. "Echo" is rejected. Mimed Guard: Mirror Barrier. Guard is the physical block, and this is a Barrier. Breaker (Net Squad role): Mauler. Breaker is the parry class, and he carries a maul.
New in v3.2, checked against these rules: the rarity tiers (Stocked, Found, Hoarded, Drifted, Sought, Rumored, Storied; "Guarded" was rejected because Guard is the physical block), the potion bands (Dregs, Weak, Sound, Potent, Pure), the collecting inks (Glimpsed, Marked, Known) and witness classes (Standing, Drifting, Sealed), Stanch, the Hazard category, Trust XP, the Curl glyph (not "Drift", which names the Drift), and the Apothecary (so "alchemist" stays the player's word).
New in v3.3, checked against these rules: the starter spells (Cinder, Gust, Frostmote, Mana Sense, Firebolt, Shard, Arc, Wind Slash, Stonegrip, Fireball, Ice Lance, Torrent), Chill, Flinch, Block drain and Barrier collapse (4.13). Design-only terms: settle window and release lag. Missing systems: the guild ranks (Leader, Officer, Member, Initiate), the Registrar, the Spar, the Guild Hall, Wellstone sizes (Small, Large), the glyphs Thrift and Patience, the materials (Ironstone, Deepsilver, Leystone, Hide, Golem Core Shard, Glyph Source, Starmetal), the channel classes (Shallows, Narrows, Open channel), Take Stock, Body captions, family likeness. Bestiary: the creatures (Glutmaw Hound, Dolmen Bruin, Loomhorn, Mirrorplate Sentinel, Cairn Walker, Latch Crab, Candle Wraith, the Unburied, the Drowned, Blister Pod, Sap Ooze, Lantern Nettle, Driftwalker, Glyph Heron, the Bound Archivist, Anvilback, Gloamwing, the Mourner, the Choir, the Hinge, Lantern-Eater, the Unformed, the Weft, the Wader, the Likeness, the Thinling, the Watch, the Marshal), the squads and roles (Net, Rust, Lance and Reach Squads; Netters, Mauler, Snuffer, Rasps, Spiller, Grappler, Lancers, Tracker, Warden, Polemen), the Marshal's law words (Writ, Sentence, Gavel, Arrest, Verdict, Named), the creature spells (Tidebolt, Leech, Rimewall, Sunfall, Shardstorm, Cairnfall, Unmaking), and the mechanic words Glut, Vent, Emptied, Phase, half-manifest, Singe, Soaked, Stung and Root. Owner renames (v3.3), one name, one meaning: Burn (status): Singe, since Burn names Constitution's Burn (3.5) and Mana Burn (3.1). Caster (Reach Squad role): Lobber, since Caster is a role mechanic (14.1). Hold Fast (the Watch): Clinch, since it read against the Holdfast and the spell Hold (4.11). Wick (creature spell, e1_wick): Leech (e1_leech), since Wick is a town (27.3). Spent (Mana Beast state): Emptied, since the Spent are the Seers' order (27.1).
World and lore (v3.3, 27), renames of the proposal's own earlier names: the Wake (archipelago): the Holdfast. "Wake" reads as borrowed and is one unsearchable word; a holdfast is what the land is, held fast. Caldermere: Longmere. "Calder" is a proper name; a long lake is a long mere. The Calder (leyline): the Mereline. One name, one meaning: the leyline under the mere. Greywick: Ashwick, and the Grey (Dead Zone): the Ash. "Grey" names Grey Health (3.2) and the Grey Marble (10.4), and it ties to Ash Loaf (18.6). The Still Room: the Deaf Room. "Still" names Still Aura (7.3). Ambit: Tollstead. Latinate; a toll is a count, a stead is a place. Ferrow: Firmbank. An invented word; the town stands on the one firm bank. Harrow Hall: Steelhall. The Steel Vow is sworn there. Garn Ashwick: Garn Cole, since the island took the name. Odo Fell: Odo Reed. "Fell" collided with Thornfell.
Working title (v3.3, 27.11): Untitled Mage Game: Moorings (working title). Holdfast was rejected as the title because it is already the title of an existing game; it stays the archipelago's name. Thrice is the fallback. Volition (Latinate), The Held (opaque) and Wake (borrowed association) were dropped.
New in v3.3, world and lore (27), checked against these rules: the Holdfast; the regions Longmere, Ashwick, Thornfell and Lanternmarsh; the Mereline and the Ash; the towns Highwater, Wick, Tollstead and Firmbank; the Outer Shoals; the set pieces (the Weirs, the Weir Towers, the Drowned Stair, the Glassfield, Steelhall, the Drinking Stones, the Deaf Room, the Salt Road, the Counting Stair, the Pin, the Quiet Gallery, the Wraith Terraces, the Lantern Field, the Standing Stones, the Gallows Alder); the schools and peoples (the Tally, the Kindled, the Struck, the Plainsteel, the stonehanded, the Held, the Spent); the roles (Readers, Trackers, ferry pilots, the lamplighter, the shoal hermit); the NPCs (Pell, the Ostrey family, Idren Holt, Brannoch Teague, Sera Ostrey, Odo Reed, Garn Cole, Teodor Weir, the starmetal smith); the shard names (Ebb, Neap, Slack, Swell, Chop, Lull, Fetch, Lee); and the in-world words grain, hollow and thin ground. Cleared by the owner (v3.3): the five mechanic collisions were renamed (Singe, Lobber, Clinch, Leech, Emptied; see the bestiary line above). Place and creature names that share a plain word (Lanternmarsh, the Lantern Field, the Lantern glyph, the Lantern Nettle and Lantern-Eater; the Drowned Stair and the Drowned; the Holdfast) are kept on purpose: none of them names a mechanic.
Kept on purpose: Echelon, Hollow, Contest, Slip, Spellcut, Winded, Strain, Grain, Ripe, the Long Road, Fissures, the Drift, Bleed Zones, Anchors, Hold the Breach, the Unbroken Hand, the tier names (Raw to Perfected), and the cooking vocabulary. These already read as plain in-world words.
